#10865. 5 Best AI Anime Video Generators in 2026, and Are AI Anime Videos Worth Making?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI video generators can now turn a prompt or a character image into a few seconds of animation, and anime is one of the most popular styles people ask for. But anime is also one of the hardest styles to get right: flat colors, thin linework and stylized motion expose the weaknesses of video models quickly. For developers and designers, the useful question is not which tool is “best”, but what each one actually offers, what it cannot do yet, and how to fit it into a real production workflow.

This article compares five generators on what their own pages state, covers the problems they all share, asks whether AI anime videos are worth making, and then explains how the models work and how designers and developers can build a practical workflow, including ffmpeg and API-integration examples. “Best” here depends on the job: the tools are listed in no ranking order, each is linked once to its official site for reference, and the comparison is based on public vendor pages reviewed in November 2026. Features, models and pricing change often, so verify current details before relying on any of them.

We reviewed each vendor’s own site and compared what it states on the same points. This is a documentation-based comparison, not a lab benchmark. Where a page does not state something, we say so instead of guessing.

AI anime video generator by Pollo AI sits within a general AI video platform. On its main site, Pollo describes generating video from text prompts or uploaded images and editing existing videos, such as adding, changing, removing or restyling content. It also offers AI image generation, so a character image and the animation can be made in one place.

Strengths for anime work: text-to-video and image-to-video in the same tool, plus video editing and restyling, which suits experimenting with looks.

Limitations and gaps: the pages we could read do not name the underlying models, list credit amounts or pricing, or describe anime-specific controls such as reference characters or first and last frame inputs. You will need to test consistency yourself.

Fits: creators who want image, video and editing tools in one platform and are comfortable testing results before committing.

Vidu lists text-to-video, image-to-video and reference-to-video modes. For image-to-video you can set first and last frames. Reference-to-video lets you upload several images of a character, object or scene, and saved references can be reused through a My References feature. The site highlights 2D animation and diverse artistic styles, mentions 1080p output and offers an API platform aimed at enterprise use.

Strengths for anime work: the clearest set of consistency tools among the five pages we reviewed, with first and last frames, multi-image references and reusable saved characters.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not specify clip durations beyond examples, detail its anime-specific templates, or list prices. Free credits are mentioned without an amount.

Fits: sequences built around a recurring character, and teams that want an API.

Kling AI presents Kling 4.0 for video, described as offering stable dynamic motion and an audio-visual experience, alongside an image model, a Motion Control tool and a node-based Canvas editor for working through scripts, storyboards, images and video. It also lists MCP and CLI support for batch generation and automated production, and an API platform with documentation.

Strengths for anime work: motion-focused tooling and a workflow environment for storyboards and multi-step production, plus automation options for developers.

Limitations and gaps: the page we reviewed does not detail image-to-video specifics, first and last frame controls, camera control, durations, video resolution or pricing, and it gives no anime-specific guidance.

Fits: teams that value motion control and scripted or batch pipelines.

Hailuo AI, from MiniMax, describes creating AI videos and images from text or photos. Its page shows a reference field and an Omni Reference option, a banner for a model that mentions multimodal generation and editing, and many photo-based templates such as photo-to-motion, transformation and style transfer.

Strengths for anime work: an accessible, template-driven way to animate a still image and try stylized effects quickly.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not explain how reference works, or state camera control, durations, resolution, API availability or pricing. Template-driven results may be harder to control precisely than prompt- and frame-driven workflows.

Fits: quick experiments and social clips made from a single image.

Dreamina describes itself as an all-in-one creative platform for images, videos and talking avatars. For video it lists text-to-video, image-to-video, sketch-to-video, cartoon video generation and reference-to-video, using several Seedance models, along with editing examples such as green-screen replacement. It also offers image generation and upscaling.

Strengths for anime work: a broad toolset, including cartoon-oriented generation, references and editing in one environment.

Limitations and gaps: no official maximum duration or video resolution is stated, and the page notes that displayed pricing is for reference only and that final pricing requires signing in. The breadth of features and models can make it harder to know which one to use for a given shot.

Fits: creators who want images, video and avatars under one roof.

“Not stated” means the vendor page we reviewed did not say, not that the feature does not exist. Treat the table as a checklist of questions to answer in your own tests.

It depends on the job and on what you compare them with. Against a full studio production they are limited. Against having no animation at all, they change what a small team or solo creator can attempt.

Treat the generator as a fast prototyping and shot-making assistant, not a replacement for writing, direction and design. Audience reaction also varies, and some viewers are openly critical of AI-made work, so quality and disclosure both matter.

Most current generators are diffusion models extended to video. As in image diffusion, the model starts from random noise and removes it step by step, guided by your inputs. The extension to video, described in research such as Video Diffusion Models, is that the model denoises a whole sequence of frames together, so that motion and appearance stay coherent over time. Many systems work in a compressed latent space rather than on raw pixels to keep generation affordable.

Create an original character sheet with front, side and three-quarter views, a fixed color palette and a clear silhouette. Keep it simple: fewer fine details means less flicker.

Break the sequence into short shots with one action and one camera move each. A four-shot, twenty-second sequence is more realistic than one long generation.

Start each shot from a clean keyframe of your character. Where a tool supports first and last frames or reference images, use them to define the pose and keep identity stable.

Describe subject, action, camera and mood, and add a style anchor. For example: “The girl slowly turns toward the camera, wind moves her hair, petals drift past, gentle push-in, 2D cel shading, clean linework.”

Scrub through each clip for warped hands, drifting faces and flickering lines. Discard or regenerate failures instead of hoping nobody notices.

Join the best takes, color-match them, add sound and captions, and add disclosure where a platform requires it.

Developers can make the pipeline repeatable. Two patterns help: scripted post-processing with ffmpeg, and a resilient job-based client for any generation API.

The script below extracts a last frame to seed the next shot, normalizes clips to a common size and frame rate so they can be joined, concatenates them, and optionally holds each frame twice for an “on twos” look. Adjust sizes and frame rates to your project.

Video generation is usually asynchronous: you submit a job, then poll or receive a callback. The sketch below shows the pattern with a timeout and backoff. The endpoints and fields are placeholders, so follow your provider’s documentation for real ones.

Most are diffusion models for video. They start from noise and remove it step by step across a sequence of frames, guided by a text prompt and often a starting image or reference images, until coherent motion appears.

Usually, for consistency. The image locks the character and style, while the prompt only needs to describe motion.

Thin lines and flat color regions make small frame-to-frame changes visible. Simpler designs, slower motion and post-processing reduce it.

Partly. Reference images, saved characters and shared keyframes help, but you should still review every shot for drift.

For short clips, concept previews and storyboards, often yes. For long, dialogue-driven stories, not yet.

It depends on the tool and plan. Read the current terms and make sure you have rights to any characters or assets you upload.

Run the same character image and prompt through each, then compare consistency, line stability, motion quality and cost per usable second.

AI anime video generators are useful, but uneven. The five tools reviewed here differ in input modes, consistency tools, developer access and how much their own pages say, and none removes the core problems of consistency, flicker and limited acting. The practical approach is to test several tools on the same shots, keep your workflow tool-agnostic, design characters that are simple and consistent, and review every clip before it ships. Used that way, they are a capable prototyping and shot-making aid, not a substitute for design and direction.

#10862. The ultimate monitor for CS2 and Valorant: What actually matters?

In competitive shooters like Counter-Strike 2, Valorant, and Call of Duty, every millisecond counts. Players are constantly looking for ways to get an edge, whether that’s upgrading their PC, tweaking settings, or investing in a faster monitor.

But while graphics cards usually get most of the attention, your monitor plays a huge role in how smooth and responsive a game feels. After all, there’s only so much benefit to pushing hundreds of frames per second if your display can’t keep up.

With gaming monitors now hitting 480Hz and beyond, refresh rates are reaching some pretty astonishing numbers. But you might be asking, can you actually feel the difference, and is upgrading from 144Hz or 240Hz really worth it?

In competitive shooters, where quick reactions and precise aiming matter, a monitor’s refresh rate can influence how smoothly movement appears on screen.

A higher refresh rate allows the display to update its image more frequently. For example, a 144Hz monitor refreshes approximately every 6.94 milliseconds, while a 480Hz display refreshes every 2.08 milliseconds.

That difference can help players track moving opponents and receive updated visual information sooner, particularly in fast-paced games like Counter-Strike 2 and Valorant.

However, the benefits become smaller at higher refresh rates, and players need a PC capable of producing enough frames to take full advantage of them.

Refresh rate isn’t the only factor affecting motion clarity, either. OLED panels generally offer much faster pixel response times than traditional LCD monitors, reducing the ghosting that can appear behind moving objects.

Why you should upgrade to an OLED for GTA 6

The best gaming monitor in 2024: Budget, 4K & Ultrawide

The best refresh rate for Counter-Strike 2

GIGABYTE’s AORUS ELITE lineup combines those fast response times with high refresh rates, offering different options depending on how players use their monitors.

The AORUS FO32U24G features a 32-inch fourth-generation Tandem WOLED panel with a 0.03ms GTG response time. Its Dual Mode technology allows players to switch between two display configurations:

This approach could appeal to players who regularly switch between different genres. Someone playing Counter-Strike 2 might prefer the 480Hz mode, while a player enjoying a visually demanding single-player game could switch back to 4K.

The smaller AORUS FO27Q28G takes a different approach, offering a 27-inch QHD Tandem WOLED panel with a 280Hz refresh rate and the same advertised 0.03ms GTG response time.

Both monitors also support NVIDIA G-SYNC compatibility and AMD FreeSync Premium Pro, which can help reduce tearing when frame rates fluctuate.

Refresh rate and response time aren’t the only specifications to focus on.

Modern gaming monitors increasingly include built-in features designed to give players more control over visibility, screen layout, and the information displayed during gameplay.

GIGABYTE’s AORUS ELITE monitors include several Tactical Features intended for different competitive scenarios:

Some of these features may be particularly relevant to Counter-Strike 2 players, many of whom have specific preferences for screen size, resolution, and aspect ratio.

Tactical Switch 2.0 allows players to change between different display configurations without manually adjusting every setting, while Tactical HUD could be useful in games where players regularly need to reference mini-maps or other on-screen information.

GIGABYTE also includes Night Vision and AI Black Equalizer to improve visibility in darker environments, alongside Ultra Clear, which uses black-frame insertion to reduce perceived motion blur.

Not every feature will be useful to every player, and some display-side assists may be restricted under competitive tournament rules. Players should check the rules of the games and competitions they participate in before using them.

Although 480Hz monitors offer extremely frequent display updates, not every competitive player needs the highest available refresh rate.

For someone upgrading from a 60Hz or 75Hz display, even moving to 144Hz can make a substantial difference in perceived smoothness.

Players already using 240Hz or 280Hz monitors may notice a smaller improvement when switching to 480Hz, particularly if their PC struggles to maintain the frame rates required to take advantage of it.

A 480Hz monitor makes the most sense for players who prioritize competitive performance and have a PC capable of producing extremely high frame rates. For others, a 280Hz OLED may provide a better balance between responsiveness, resolution, and everyday use.

GIGABYTE’s M series offers several alternatives depending on panel technology, screen finish, and gaming preferences:

The MO27Q2A provides a QD-OLED alternative for players who want fast response times and high refresh rates, while the MO27Q28G and MO27Q28GR offer different screen finishes depending on the environment.

The M27UP takes a different approach with an IPS panel, making it an option for players who want 4K gaming and high-refresh competitive performance without moving to OLED.

For players considering OLED, long-term panel care is another factor worth keeping in mind.

Competitive shooters frequently display static interface elements, including health bars, ammunition counters, mini-maps, and scoreboards. Over extended periods, these elements can contribute to uneven pixel wear.

GIGABYTE’s AORUS ELITE FO32U24G and FO27Q28G feature AI OLED Care Pro, which includes automated pixel cleaning, brightness adjustments, and display protection designed to reduce burn-in risk.

The MO27Q2A, MO27Q28G, and MO27Q28GR instead feature AI OLED Care, providing background panel maintenance and protection.

While 480Hz OLED monitors represent a high-performance option for competitive gaming, they aren’t necessarily the right choice for everyone.

Players who spend most of their time in Counter-Strike 2 or Valorant and have PCs capable of producing hundreds of frames per second may benefit from the additional responsiveness.

For those who split their time between competitive shooters and visually demanding single-player games, a lower-refresh OLED with higher resolution may make more sense.

Ultimately, the best gaming monitor isn’t always the one with the highest refresh rate. It’s the one that matches the games you play, the hardware you own, and the experience you’re trying to achieve.

#10861. Exploring the Indium phosphide supply chain — the material constraint tightening the optical interconnect bottleneck

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

As the focus goes in on Optical, InP comes under the spotlight.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Indium phosphide (InP) is a compound material used in specialized chip-making. Unlike silicon, which is the fundamental building block for mainstream logic and memory chips, InP — made from indium and phosphorus — is mainly used to manufacture highly specialized photonic and high-frequency electronic chips, largely due to its outstanding optical properties. Its immensely expensive and complex manufacturing process also makes it impractical for the large volumes and die sizes needed for logic and memory chips. With the industry predicting a shortfall of this crucial material right as the shift to optical interconnects in data centers begins, we explore the current state of the supply chain.

InP chips have powered high-speed lasers, modulators, photodetectors, and various other optical components used across the telecommunications industry and defense for decades. While the InP supply chain was relatively small and highly specialized, it was generally stable and sufficient to meet global demands.

Then came the AI boom. As AI clusters hit the physical limits of moving enormous amounts of data over copper, the industry began the switch to optical interconnects. First came ever-faster optical transceivers. Now the industry is transitioning to silicon photonics, near-packaged optics, and co-packaged optics, bringing optical links progressively closer to the processors themselves. InP is central to much of this transition, building the critical active optical components needed to turn electrical data into light and back again.

Individual AI data centers are already being built around hundreds of thousands of accelerators, with upcoming gigawatt-scale buildouts expected to house millions. Connecting those processors translates into demand for millions upon millions of indispensable optical components, all relying on the same relatively tiny InP supply chain.

Japan's Sumitomo Electric and U.S.-headquartered AXT together account for almost 80% of global InP substrate manufacturing, with JX Advanced Metals adding roughly another 10%. AXT, however, manufactures most of its InP substrates in China, where exports now require government licenses, further exacerbating the situation. It gets worse: China accounts for around 70% of global refined indium output, which is recovered as a byproduct of processing zinc. Export controls mean that restrictions apply to both the material itself and the finished wafer.

The resulting squeeze is already sending ripples across the industry, with several companies predicting shortfalls. Michael Hurlston, CEO of optical-component manufacturer Lumentum, said in July that the company's InP fabs were shipping more than 30% below customer demand, despite rapid capacity expansion. Coherent also described InP capacity as the “primary constraint” on its transceiver business.

A simple solution would be to expand the supply chain. However, growing large, low-defect InP crystals is difficult, while qualifying new suppliers requires time that doesn't align with the industry's demand. Furthermore, expanding substrate and laser-fabrication capacity can take years. Meanwhile, to secure optical components capacity for its accelerators, Nvidia has invested $2 billion each in Lumentum and Coherent, attaching multibillion-dollar purchase commitments and future capacity rights to both deals. Both companies, along with several others in the supply chain, are frantically pursuing solutions.

Indium phosphide belongs to the III-V family of compound semiconductors, named after the periodic-table groups of their constituent elements; in InP’s case, indium and phosphorus. Its better-known relatives include gallium arsenide and gallium nitride.

Indium phosphide’s distinguishing property, which separates it from silicon, is its direct bandgap, measured at about 1.34 eV at room temperature. In a direct-bandgap material, electrons dropping across the gap release their energy efficiently as photons, making the material particularly useful for lasers and other active optical devices.

Silicon, on the other hand, has an indirect bandgap, meaning that the same transition requires an additional interaction with a crystal lattice and mostly sheds its energy as heat instead. Therefore, while the material can — in silicon photonics — guide, split, and modulate light using structures fabricated with mature CMOS processes, it does not produce the light beam. As a result, Modern silicon-photonics systems commonly rely on a III-V laser source, most commonly based on the InP material system, to provide the light that the silicon circuitry manipulates.

The fiber-optic wavelength window’s alignment with InP's properties is another reason for the material's ubiquity. Modern telecom and data-center links commonly operate around 1,310 nm and 1,550 nm, where optical fiber exhibits favorable transmission characteristics. Pure InP itself does not emit at those exact wavelengths. However, InGaAsP and InAlGaAs alloys, which are grown on lattice-matched InP substrates, can have their bandgaps engineered for those bands. InGaAs layers on InP are also highly effective photodetectors at telecom wavelengths.

These characteristics and functionalities make the InP platform critical to many active functions in optical communications, including distributed-feedback (DFB) lasers, electro-absorption modulated lasers (EMLs), continuous-wave (CW) lasers, semiconductor optical amplifiers, photodetectors, and complete photonic integrated circuits. Silicon photonics and InP consequently complement each other. Silicon provides scalable optical circuitry, while InP provides efficient light generation and several high-performance active optical devices.

Beyond photonics, InP is also commonly used in high-frequency electronic chips. Electrons move through the material far faster than through silicon, giving it strong high-frequency performance that makes it suitable for millimeter-wave and terahertz electronics, radar receivers, and high-speed data converters. This market is much smaller than photonics, but it has few substitutes at the top of the frequency range.

InP lasers and transceivers have been converting electrical signals into light for long-haul fiber networks, metro links, submarine cables, and eventually shorter-reach data-center connections for decades. Despite 5G rollouts driving demand higher, the indium phosphide supply chain remained a stable, moderately sized business with predictable growth. The AI boom has now driven demand up by several orders of magnitude.

Modern AI clusters can contain hundreds of thousands of interconnected accelerators that continuously exchange data. Copper remains a cheap, effective connector over short distances, but electrical losses, signal integrity, and power requirements progressively worsen as bandwidth and distance increase. This has led to the rapid adoption of optical interconnects.

In this setup, a modulator encodes electrical data from the switch or accelerator onto light from a laser. Fiber cables then carry it across the link to a photodetector that converts the received photons back into an electrical signal. In pluggable transceivers, many of these functions are integrated into a compact module plugged into the front of a network switch, with InP-based components executing several steps in the process.

Lumentum's 200G EML, for example, monolithically combines a wavelength-locked DFB laser and an electro-absorption modulator on its InP platform, and is designed for 200-Gbps-per-lane links used in 1.6T transceivers. Coherent is producing InP EMLs, high-power CW lasers, and high-speed photodiodes for the same generation of hardware. As aggregate link speeds move from 400G and 800G to 1.6T and beyond, the number and performance of these InP-based optical components become increasingly important.

At the same time, the industry is bringing optics right to the board with co-packaged optics (CPO) to further shorten the distance data travels. Conventional pluggable optics leave around 15 to 30 centimeters (5.90 to 11.81 inches) of high-speed copper trace between the switch ASIC and the optical module. CPO brings the optical engine onto the accelerator package as a photonic integrated circuit (PIC) chip, shrinking that electrical path and reducing power consumption. Nvidia's Quantum-X and Spectrum-X photonics switches are among the first major AI platforms built around that approach.

While they integrate several optical functions onto the boards, PIC chips do not generate the laser. CPO and silicon-photonics designs still mostly use external InP CW lasers to supply light to the silicon photonic circuits, further consolidating the demand for indium phosphide in AI data centers and the industry as a whole.

As a result of the enormous, rapidly expanding market for the material, indium phosphide’s supply chain is constrained at several points, starting with the raw material itself. Unlike many metals which are mined from dedicated deposits, indium is typically recovered as a trace byproduct of processing zinc sulfide ore. Manufacturers then combine high-purity indium and phosphorus and grow them into a single-crystal InP ingot, or boule, which they process into substrate wafers with tightly controlled properties. Next, manufacturers grow extremely thin epitaxial layers of InP-related compounds over it, commonly using metal-organic chemical vapor deposition. The epiwafer then goes through several chip-making processes and module assembly to become optical chips and devices.

Indium production's dependence on Zinc processing creates bottlenecks in InP's supply chain, right at the beginning of the stream. The USGS put global primary refined indium production at roughly 1,100 tonnes in 2025, with China accounting for 760 tonnes. For context, the BBC estimates that 30,000 tonnes of electronic-grade silicon used exclusively to make microchips are mined yearly. The United States imports all of the InP it consumes, producing nothing. Meanwhile, indium substrate manufacturers Japan's Sumitomo and JX Metals source more than 90% of their high-purity indium feedstock from China, meaning even non-Chinese substrate makers begin their process with Chinese material.

Further downstream, at the substrate manufacturing level, Sumitomo Electric holds roughly 40% of the global InP substrate market, U.S.-based AXT around 35%, and JX Advanced Metals about 10%. Together, the three control more than 90% of capacity. Although AXT is headquartered in California, most of its InP substrate production runs through its Beijing Tongmei subsidiary, which puts it directly under China's export licensing regime. Other fringe substrate manufacturers include Freiberger Compound Materials in Germany and a growing set of Chinese entrants, including Yunnan Germanium.

The supply chain is also highly concentrated at the device and component level, with Coherent and Lumentum dominating Western laser and component production. IQE supplies epitaxial wafers, and Japanese firms, including Mitsubishi Electric, supply laser components. Chinese module makers such as Innolight and Eoptolink assemble a large share of the world's finished 800G modules, pairing Western lasers with Broadcom and Marvell DSPs. We recently explored how this entanglement complicates any attempt to decouple the two ecosystems, even as the FCC is considering a ban on Chinese optical hardware. Coherent primarily sources substrates from AXT, while Lumentum relies heavily on Sumitomo and JX.

On the technical side, wafer-size limitations in InP semiconductors add another constraint. While silicon logic and memory moved to 300-mm wafers in the early 2000s, the InP industry is only now transitioning from 3-inch to 6-inch (150-mm) production; primarily because the material is brittle, expensive, and grown in small boules, factors that limit wafer size. Doubling the wafer diameter gives four times the area. Coherent's six-inch line, for example, yields more than four times as many devices as its three-inch line at a lower cost per die. On the other hand, moving a fab to six-inch requires new crystal-growth recipes, new handling equipment, new process qualification, and extensive customer requalification.

The supply chain was already struggling under these constraints when, in February 2025, Beijing placed InP, trimethylindium, triethylindium, and the technology used to produce them under export controls, requiring licenses for overseas shipments. AXT called the export controls its most significant current challenge, noting that even with sufficient capacity and strong demand, its ability to ship outside China depends on Chinese licensing decisions.

The widening gap between demand and InP industry output points to a fast-approaching shortfall. Coherent first warned of an InP shortage on its May earnings call. At the close of its fiscal year in August, CEO Jim Anderson described indium phosphide availability as the company's primary constraint, confirming that transceiver assembly and test capacity were available.

Lumentum CEO Hurlston said in July that telecom customers historically ordered lasers in the hundreds, while Nvidia and the hyperscalers were asking for hundreds of millions. Despite operating multiple InP fabs, Lumentum was shipping more than 30% below customer requirements. Earlier in the year, Hurlston said the company's capacity was sold out through 2027 and probably into 2028, even with the expanded output. In both cases, capacity was mostly bought out via Nvidia’s $4 billion investment.

To ease the multifaceted bottleneck, the industry is pursuing several solutions simultaneously, primarily expansions. Coherent is expanding its Sherman, Texas campus, reportedly described as the first and largest high-volume 6-inch InP semiconductor manufacturing facility. Washington signed a letter of intent in June for up to $50 million in CHIPS Act funding for the expansion. Coherent is now on track to double internal InP output in 2026, one quarter ahead of its original plan, and more than double it again by the end of 2027, while also adding 6-inch production in Sweden and Zurich.

Lumentum, meanwhile, is converting a 240,000-square-foot former Qorvo gallium-arsenide facility in North Carolina, which it bought for about $18 million, into another InP fab. The relevance of these expansions largely depends on the downstream InP substrate supply. Lumentum signed an agreement in July to reserve AXT InP substrate capacity through 2031, with an initial $43.5 million deposit and another $43.5 million payment contemplated for 2028. Coherent signed its own three-year 6-inch supply agreement with AXT a month earlier and prepaid $22.3 million against committed capacity.

The substrate suppliers are also expanding in kind. AXT is channeling fresh funds into Beijing Tongmei to double capacity by the end of 2026 and double it again in 2027. Similarly, JX Advanced Metals plans to invest roughly ¥120 billion ($746 million) through FY2030 to lift capacity seven to ten times above FY2025 levels, its largest-ever commitment in semiconductor materials.

Indium production — the uppermost stage of the stream — is far less active, with the biggest hurdle being the reliance on Zinc production. Global zinc mine production hasn't grown materially since 2011, sitting at or below 13 million tonnes a year, according to analysts. Wood Mackenzie expects just 0.1% growth in 2026. On the other hand, not everyone expects the crunch to last. LightCounting estimated in April that optical component demand exceeded supply by about 30% but projected that shortages would largely clear by the end of 2026 as new capacity comes online.

There are also viable, albeit slow, technical workarounds to ease the crunch. Heterogeneous integration bonds InP onto silicon to combine the strengths of both, while quantum dot lasers offer better temperature stability for uncooled operation. Furthermore, researchers continue to push InP quantum dot structures grown directly on silicon substrates. While these do not remove indium from the equation, they change how much each device needs and how it attaches, reducing consumption. Photonics is widely viewed as one of AI's major bottlenecks. However, the indium phosphide supply chain is itself tightening that bottleneck.

#10859. NASA’s new AI space chip could let spacecraft think for themselves

NASA is developing a powerful new computer chip designed to dramatically increase the intelligence and performance of future spacecraft. Through a commercial partnership, the project is creating advanced processing technology capable of helping spacecraft operate more independently during missions far from Earth.

NASA's High Performance Spaceflight Computing project is focused on boosting the computing capabilities of spacecraft used in space exploration. Current missions rely on older processors because they are durable enough to survive the extreme conditions of space. While those chips are dependable, they lack the performance needed for more advanced missions.

The agency says newer and far more capable processors are essential for future autonomous spacecraft, faster onboard scientific analysis, and supporting astronauts during missions to the Moon and Mars.

"Building on the legacy of previous space processors, this new multicore system is fault-tolerant, flexible, and extremely high-performing," said Eugene Schwanbeck, program element manager in NASA's Game Changing Development program at the agency's Langley Research Center, in Hampton, Virginia. "NASA's commitment to advancing spaceflight computing is a triumph of technical achievement and collaboration."

Radiation Hardened Processor Faces Extreme Testing

At the center of the project is a new radiation-hardened processor built to deliver up to 100 times the computing power of today's spaceflight computers while surviving the harsh environment of space. Engineers at NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) in Southern California are running a wide range of tests designed to simulate those conditions.

"We are putting these new chips through the wringer by carrying out radiation, thermal, and shock tests while also evaluating their performance through a rigorous functional test campaign," said Jim Butler, High Performance Space Computing project manager at JPL.

To qualify for spaceflight, the processor must withstand intense electromagnetic radiation and dramatic temperature fluctuations that can damage electronics. High-energy particles from the Sun and deep space can also trigger computer errors that force spacecraft into "safe mode," temporarily shutting down nonessential systems until engineers resolve the issue.

NASA is also testing how the chip handles the challenges of planetary landings.

"To simulate real-world performance, we are using high-fidelity landing scenarios from real NASA missions that would typically require power-intensive hardware to process huge volumes of landing-sensor data," said Butler. "This is an exciting time for us to be working on hardware that will enable NASA's next giant leaps."

Testing at JPL began in February and is expected to continue for several months. Early results have been highly encouraging. According to NASA, the processor is functioning as intended and has shown performance levels roughly 500 times greater than the radiation-hardened chips currently used in spacecraft.

The team also marked the beginning of testing with a symbolic moment by sending an email titled "Hello Universe," referencing the famous introductory messages used during the early days of computer programming.

AI Powered Spacecraft and Deep Space Missions

The processor is being developed jointly by JPL and Microchip Technology Inc., based in Chandler, Arizona. The company is working with NASA through a commercial partnership, and sample chips have already been shared with defense and commercial aerospace partners.

The technology is expected to play a major role in the future of autonomous spacecraft. With onboard artificial intelligence, spacecraft could respond to unexpected situations in real time when communication delays make human control impractical. The chip could also help deep space missions process, store, and transmit massive amounts of scientific data back to Earth more efficiently.

NASA says the processor may eventually support crewed missions to the Moon and Mars as well.

Small Processor With Massive Computing Power

The device is known as a system-on-a-chip (or SoC), meaning it combines the essential components of a computer into a single compact unit. The processor includes central processing units, computational offloads, advanced networking systems, memory, and input/output interfaces.

SoCs are widely used in smartphones and tablets because they are compact and energy efficient. However, NASA's version is designed to survive for years in deep space, potentially traveling millions (or even billions) of miles from Earth without maintenance or repairs.

Once the processor is certified for use in space, NASA plans to integrate it into a wide variety of missions, including Earth orbiters, planetary rovers, deep space probes, and crewed habitats.

The technology could also have benefits on Earth. Microchip plans to adapt the processor for industries such as aviation and automotive manufacturing.

The project is managed by the Space Technology Mission Directorate's Game Changing Development (GCD) program at NASA Langley. The GCD program and JPL, which is managed by Caltech in Pasadena, California, oversaw the development process from mission planning and industry studies through final delivery.

NASA JPL selected Microchip as a partner in 2022, and the company funded its own research and development work on the processor.

#10856. Grand Theft Auto 6 review: Is this the best game ever?

Grand Theft Auto 6 is the greatest open-world simulation ever created. It is a breathtaking recreation of modern America, with extraordinary attention to detail and unrivalled freedom.

Vice City and the sprawling state of Leonida feel alive in ways no game has achieved before. Every street, random character, and minor interaction contributes to a world that feels like it exists beyond the player. It’s a technical achievement that raises the bar for open-world games forever.

For all its spectacle, ambition, and criminal excess, GTA 6 is also a deeply human story about two people trying to survive together in a world determined to tear them apart. And it’s the combination of unprecedented scale and genuine emotional depth that makes Rockstar’s latest masterpiece so extraordinary.

Grand Theft Auto 6 follows the story of Jason and Lucia, both fighting to make a name for themselves in Leonida after falling on hard times. We meet the two characters years before the events of the game, as they come across each other in Vice City, both stealing the same car.

Beyond that, Lucia is searching for answers about her family, mainly her child, after she lost custody following a stint in prison. As well as trying to pay the bills while working for Brian Heder, Jason is trying to find himself after serving in the army.

To both get what they’re looking for, they must build their way up from petty thieves to big-time bank robbers, all while dealing with what Leonida has to throw at them – it can get pretty wild, too.

Red Dead Redemption 2 was, for me, the greatest game ever made. Arthur Morgan’s story still remains my favorite in gaming, but what really made RDR2 special was the immersion. The Wild West felt totally real, making even the most ordinary activities enjoyable.

GTA 6 takes everything that made RDR2 so impressive and brings it to a modern-day recreation of Florida. It feels as though Rockstar took the best elements of Red Dead Redemption 2 and GTA 5 and amplified everything. The result is easily the most detailed open world ever created and quite possibly the best game of all time.

Leonida is absolutely packed with things to do. Whether you’re exploring the wilderness, discovering new locations, encountering wildlife, or investigating mysteries, there’s always something to keep you occupied.

It feels like there’s a new random encounter waiting around every corner, and in true Rockstar fashion, the developer seems to have thought of just about everything.

Better yet, you’re rewarded for going off the beaten path. There’s so much to discover that simply taking a detour can be just as entertaining as progressing through the main story. This is a world that begs you to explore every inch of it, and it’s remarkably easy to lose track of what you originally set out to do.

The attention to detail is on another level, too. Even everyday objects like books get more care than you’d expect, weapons can be customized to your heart’s content, and there are countless interactions that reward players for their creativity.

For instance, there’s one encounter early on where an NPC is filling his truck with gas. Players can flick a lit cigarette in his direction, triggering a massive gas station explosion.

Visually, GTA 6 is spectacular and as incredible as Vice City looks, the surrounding areas are equally impressive. From the lighting and weather effects to the character models and animations, the hardware has been pushed to its limits.

There were so many times when I found myself driving down a highway or country road, simply marveling at the world. Towns feel genuinely lived in, complete with residential neighborhoods, commercial districts, strip malls, and a staggering number of interiors to explore. Even the traffic strikes a great balance between feeling authentic but never becoming so congested that it takes the fun out of driving.

The sheer amount happening on screen at any given moment is astonishing, especially considering the game’s graphical fidelity. There is a trade-off, however, with GTA 6 targeting 30 FPS on consoles. It’s disappointing not to have a smoother option, but given what has been accomplished with this world, it’s a compromise I’m willing to accept.

The sound design is superb, too. The bustling streets of Vice City are alive with traffic, conversations, and background noise, while the quieter areas of Leonida have their own distinct atmosphere. The radio stations are another highlight, with a fantastic selection of music that makes even a leisurely drive across the map an enjoyable experience in its own right.

Thankfully, GTA 6 is every bit as fun to play as it is to explore.

The missions are some of the best in a GTA game, with plenty of variety and outrageous moments that make the campaign a blast. There’s a great balance between the ridiculous situations we’ve come to expect from the series and the more serious story Rockstar wants to tell, without one ever undermining the other.

Much like RDR2, GTA 6 uses its early missions to introduce new mechanics while gradually building toward something greater. There are some minor pacing issues in the opening hours, particularly with some of the more mundane tasks, such as fixing up the house for Lucia. It  takes its time establishing the world and its characters while allowing players to get a feel for how the game plays.

But like any great symphony, there’s a gradual build before the crescendo. Once the training wheels start coming off midway through Chapter 2, and especially in Chapter 3, GTA 6 begins to unleash its full potential.

The revamped Wanted system, introduced relatively early on, adds an entirely new dimension to police encounters. Cops now take your appearance, the vehicle you’re driving, and whether you’re carrying weapons into account, forcing you to be much more conscious of your actions when trying to evade the law.

Playing as Jason and Lucia together is an absolute blast, thanks to some of the best teammate AI I’ve ever experienced in a game. If you’re using a suppressed weapon, your partner will do the same. You can even work together to line up synchronized headshots.

This isn’t some scripted interaction that requires a button press or an on-screen prompt like you’d find in an FPS campaign – the AI simply does it. Whether you’re controlling Jason or Lucia, it genuinely feels like you have a competent partner in crime and if you get caught or killed, it’s usually your own fault.

Perhaps my favorite aspect of the gameplay, though, is the focus on robberies. They’ve come a long way since GTA 4’s legendary Three Leaf Clover mission. We saw Rockstar expand on the concept with GTA 5 and again with RDR2, but GTA 6 takes it to another level entirely. Even an ordinary bank robbery can be as exciting as a meticulously scripted action sequence from a Call of Duty campaign or the Uncharted series.

Early on in Chapter 3, I robbed a jewelry store with both characters, broke into a safe, and made off with the loot, only to find myself in a heated police chase. I let my partner take the wheel while I tried to hold off the cops hot on our trail.

After putting some distance between ourselves and the police, but still not quite out of danger, I decided our best bet was to split up. I abandoned the main road, ran into the woods, and eventually stumbled across a farmhouse, where I stole another car and made my escape while my partner fenced the stolen goods.

What made the whole experience so memorable was that none of it felt like a carefully orchestrated mission. It was a situation I had got myself into and had to find my own way out of.

As you progress, you’ll acquire tools and skills that allow you to take a much stealthier approach, potentially pulling off robberies without the cops ever laying eyes on you.

This is fun in its own right, as completing a brilliant heist without getting caught genuinely makes you feel like a badass. However, the occasions when things don’t go according to plan make for some of the most riveting gameplay GTA 6 has to offer.

There’s a very real sense of jeopardy, too. With death resulting in the loss of all the cash you’re carrying, there’s a sudden rush of adrenaline when a robbery goes sideways and you’ve got a small fortune on the line.

And that’s before even getting into the campaign’s major action set pieces, which are among the finest Rockstar has ever crafted. The finale of Chapter 1 is a particular standout and comfortably ranks among the greatest missions I’ve ever played in a video game.

Story-wise, GTA 6 had enormous shoes to fill following on from Red Dead Redemption 2. Unfortunately, neither Jason or Lucia are written with as much depth and growth as Arthur Morgan or other key members of the Van der Linde gang. A lot of that comes down to Jason’s dialogue and his personality being quite dry compared to other GTA characters.

Dylan Rourke, who voices Jason, has his moments, and without getting into spoiler territory, there are some emotional moments where he nails it. However, he seems to have had a lot less to work with than Manni L. Perez, who helps make Lucia the more compelling of the two.

That said, GTA 6’s MacGuffins do a fantastic job at keeping interest incredibly high and driving the main plot in between side missions. And we are comparing it to what I think is the greatest video game story of all time.

Leonida is the most impressive open world I’ve ever explored, the missions are fantastic, and Jason and Lucia deliver a fine story that lives up to the brilliant world around them.

After more than a decade of waiting, Rockstar has delivered something truly special. GTA 6 isn’t just a worthy successor to GTA 5 and RDR2. It sets a new standard, not just for what open-world games can achieve, but for gaming itself.

Grand Theft Auto 6 is the greatest open-world simulation ever created. It is a breathtaking recreation of modern America, with extraordinary attention to detail and unrivalled freedom.

#10854. Forget electrons, this breakthrough uses light-matter particles to power AI

Eighty years after the creation of ENIAC, the world's first general-purpose electronic computer, researchers at the University of Pennsylvania are exploring a new way to power the future of computing. Instead of relying entirely on electrons, which have formed the backbone of computers since the 1940s, scientists are now turning to light.

ENIAC, developed by Penn researchers J. Presper Eckert and John Mauchly, helped launch the modern computing era by using streams of electrons to solve complex mathematical problems. That same electronic approach still powers today's computers, smartphones, and AI systems. But as artificial intelligence grows more demanding, the limits of electron-based hardware are becoming harder to ignore.

Electrons carry an electrical charge, which creates several challenges inside modern computer chips. As they move through materials, they generate heat and face resistance that wastes energy. Those problems become even more difficult as chips grow more complex and process enormous amounts of data for AI applications.

Researchers led by Penn physicist Bo Zhen in the School of Arts & Sciences believe photons, the particles that make up light, could help solve some of these issues.

"Because they are charge-neutral and have zero rest mass, photons can carry information quickly over long distances with minimal loss, dominating communications technology," explains Li He, co-first author of a paper published in Physical Review Letters and a former postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab. "But that neutrality means they barely interact with their environment, making them bad at the sort of signal-switching logic that computers depend on."

In other words, light is excellent for carrying information quickly and efficiently, but it struggles with the switching operations needed for computing.

Combining Light and Matter for AI Computing

To overcome that problem, Zhen's team developed a special quasiparticle called an exciton-polariton. The particle forms when photons are strongly linked with electrons inside an atomically thin semiconductor material. This combination allows light to interact much more effectively, making it capable of performing the signal switching required for computing tasks.

The breakthrough could be especially important for artificial intelligence systems, which consume enormous amounts of power.

Many experimental photonic AI chips already use light to handle certain calculations at high speed. However, when these systems need to perform nonlinear activation steps, such as decision-making operations, they usually must convert light signals back into electronic ones. That conversion slows the process and increases energy use, reducing the benefits of photonic computing.

Using exciton-polaritons, the Penn researchers demonstrated all-light switching while using only about 4 quadrillionths of a joule of energy. That amount is extraordinarily small, far below the energy needed to briefly power a tiny LED light.

Toward Faster and More Efficient AI Chips

If the technology can be successfully scaled, it could lead to photonic chips capable of processing information directly from cameras without repeated conversions between light and electricity. The approach could also lower the massive energy demands of large AI systems and potentially support basic quantum computing functions on future chips.

Bo Zhen is the Jin K. Lee Presidential Associate Professor in the Department of Physics and Astronomy in the School of Arts & Sciences at the University of Pennsylvania.

Li He was a postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab in Penn Arts & Sciences. He is currently an assistant professor at Montana State University.

Additional authors on the study include Zhi Wang and Bumho Kim from the University of Pennsylvania's School of Arts & Sciences.

The research was supported by the US Office of Naval Research (N00014-20-1-2325 and N00014-21-1-2703) and the Sloan Foundation.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10853. Top 6 AI Text to Speech Tools in 2026: Features, Strengths and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Not long ago, a synthetic voice was easy to spot: flat, rushed and strangely stressed. Today, the best AI text to speech tools can read a script with natural pacing, switch accents, whisper or laugh on cue, and even speak in a voice cloned from a short recording. That makes them useful far beyond screen readers. Developers use them for product voice prompts, marketers for video voiceovers, teachers for course narration and writers for listening back to their own drafts.

This guide explains how modern text-to-speech works, what to look for when choosing a tool, and compares six options: OpenArt, ElevenLabs, Murf AI, Speechify, WellSaid Labs and Amazon Polly. Each section covers strengths, limits and the best use case. The details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing, and plans, models and prices change often, so confirm current terms before you commit.

OpenArt’s AI text to speech tool lives inside a wider creative platform. You open the Audio tab, choose Create voice-over, select a model, paste your script, pick a voice, generate, listen and download. It runs in the browser with nothing to install. According to OpenArt’s page, you can switch between models for each script, including Fish Audio Drama 3, Eleven v4 and Eleven v4 Turbo.

The direction system is what makes it practical for storytelling. You add notes in square brackets, such as [whispering], [laughs] or [pause], directly in the script. OpenArt says Fish Audio Drama 3 gives close control over single words and mid-line changes of mood, while Eleven v4 reads the whole script to infer the emotion of each line and keeps the voice steady when you regenerate a line. Preset voices are labeled by accent, gender and age, and you can upload a recording of your own voice to clone it.

Because OpenArt also makes video and background music, you can build the voiceover, visuals and soundtrack in one place. It describes a Director mode for planning stories up to five minutes, and the voiceover can also be added in any video editor.

Pricing: New accounts get 40 free credits. Paid plans add monthly credits and watermark-free downloads, and commercial rights apply on eligible paid plans. Check the pricing page for current plans.

Best for: creators who want expressive voiceovers together with AI video and music.

ElevenLabs is one of the best-known names in AI voice. Its text to speech page describes a large voice library, voice design and both instant and professional voice cloning, with the latest model, Eleven v4, positioned as the most expressive. It also lists Eleven v3, Multilingual v2 and a low-latency Flash and Turbo family aimed at real-time use, and it claims support for more than 90 languages and accents, with the exact count varying by model.

For developers, there is a text to speech API with SDKs, which makes it practical for apps, games, agents and automated pipelines. The free plan includes 10,000 credits a month, and paid plans start with Starter at $6 a month, then Creator at $22 and Pro at $99, according to the vendor page, with introductory offers on the first month.

Pricing: Free plan with 10,000 credits a month. Paid plans from $6 a month as listed on the vendor page.

Best for: expressive narration, multilingual content and apps that need a voice API.

Murf AI positions itself as a studio for voiceovers. Its page lists more than 200 voices, built with permission and partnership from professional voice actors who earn royalties, and support for 35 or more languages for text to speech, with dubbing in 40 or more. The Studio editor offers pitch, speed and intonation controls and a custom pronunciation library, which helps with brand names and technical terms.

Murf also has an API. Its page describes Falcon 2 for conversational use with time-to-first-audio under 100 milliseconds, priced at $0.01 per minute, and Gen 2 for controllable voiceovers. Gen 2’s “Say it My Way” feature lets you record your own rendition so the model reflects your tone and inflections.

Pricing: Free plan with limits. Paid plan prices are on Murf’s pricing page.

Best for: marketing videos, training and presentations where you want to fine-tune delivery.

Speechify is built around reading aloud. You paste text or upload a PDF, DOCX, TXT or EPUB file, pick a voice, adjust playback speed and listen or download the audio. Its page lists more than 1,000 voices across 60 or more languages, including several regional English accents, and the tool works in the browser and in apps for iOS, Android, Mac and Windows, plus Chrome and Edge extensions.

The use cases lean toward productivity and accessibility: studying, going through emails and documents, language learning and support for people with visual impairments, dyslexia or ADHD. A free account saves your documents and syncs them across devices.

Pricing: You can start free. Paid plans are listed on Speechify’s pricing page.

Best for: students, professionals and anyone who wants to listen to documents and articles.

WellSaid Labs focuses on professional voiceover for businesses. Every voice is built from recordings of a real voice actor who consented and is paid when the voice is used, and the voices are licensed for commercial use. Its page describes hundreds of voices across languages, accents, tones and styles.

Pronunciation control is a highlight. You can highlight a word to see suggested pronunciations and save your choice to a team library that applies to every project, and set pacing, emphasis and pronunciation line by line. WellSaid also describes a Producer product that connects AI assistants to WellSaid through MCP and its API.

Pricing: Contact sales for a quote based on your voiceover volume.

Best for: corporate training, product marketing, courses and advertising where licensing and consistency matter.

Amazon Polly is Amazon Web Services’ text to speech service, and it is aimed squarely at developers. According to AWS, it supports more than 40 languages and language variants with over 100 male and female voices, and it works through APIs in your code. It supports SSML, the W3C standard markup for phrasing, emphasis and intonation, and custom lexicons that fix the pronunciation of acronyms and terms. Output formats include MP3 and OGG.

Pricing is usage-based. AWS lists a free tier for new accounts, with usage allowances that depend on the voice engine, so estimate your character volume before you decide. A short example shows how little code a basic request takes:

The sample uses the AWS SDK for JavaScript, calls the neural engine and sends SSML so that the pause and emphasis are explicit. Adjust the region, voice and engine to match what your account supports.

Pricing: Usage-based with a free tier for new accounts. See the AWS pricing page for current rates.

Best for: apps, IVR systems, notifications and any product that needs automated speech at scale.

Treat this table as a starting point. Features and limits change often, so test two or three tools with the same script.

Modern text-to-speech systems turn written words into audio through several stages.

Newer expressive systems go further by reading the whole script to infer emotion and context, and by accepting direction in the text itself, such as a bracketed note that says to whisper. That is why a single script can produce very different reads depending on the model and the instructions.

If you use a tool with SSML support, you can make a spoken code or number far clearer:

The say-as element tells the engine to read the number digit by digit, and the break and emphasis tags shape the rhythm. Supported tags and attributes differ between engines, so check the documentation for yours.

What is the best AI text to speech tool? It depends on the job. OpenArt suits voiceovers with video and music, ElevenLabs suits expressive multilingual audio, Murf AI suits studio-style control, Speechify suits listening, WellSaid Labs suits licensed corporate work and Amazon Polly suits developers.

Is AI text to speech free? Several tools offer free plans or credits, but limits are common. Examples include OpenArt’s free credits for new accounts, ElevenLabs’ free monthly credits and Murf’s limited free plan, which has no commercial licence.

Can I use AI voices commercially? Often yes on paid plans, but the rules differ by tool. Always check the current licence before publishing.

Can AI text to speech clone my voice? Some tools support it, including OpenArt and ElevenLabs, usually from a short recording of your own voice. Only clone voices you have permission to use.

What is SSML? Speech Synthesis Markup Language is a W3C standard that lets you control pauses, emphasis, pronunciation and pitch in synthetic speech. Amazon Polly is one service that supports it.

Which tool is best for developers? Amazon Polly and ElevenLabs both offer APIs, and Murf describes API options as well. Evaluate latency, languages, pricing and SDK support for your use case.

How do I make AI voices sound less robotic? Write conversationally, add pauses and emphasis, correct pronunciations and split long scripts into short sections. Choose a model that is designed for expressive reads.

AI text to speech is now good enough for real projects, but the right tool depends on what you are making. OpenArt is a strong first stop for expressive voiceovers alongside video and music, ElevenLabs offers a deep voice library and API, Murf AI and WellSaid Labs suit polished, controlled narration, Speechify turns documents into audio and Amazon Polly fits straight into developer workflows. Test two or three with your own script, check the licence and always listen to the full result before it goes live.

Dell, Supermicro, and other server manufacturers have been told to suspend shipments to Singapore, Thailand, Malaysia, and Indonesia.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AI data centers in Singapore, Thailand, Malaysia, and Indonesia are facing trouble as Nvidia has reportedly ordered its manufacturing partners to stop shipping AI servers to the region. According to the Financial Times, Dell, Supermicro, and other firms that make AI servers using Nvidia AI GPUs have told their customers from the four countries that the AI chip maker has forbidden them to ship their orders. The move comes as the U.S. closed the loophole that let Chinese AI companies rent compute from data centers outside China equipped with advanced AI chips sourced from American semiconductor manufacturers like Nvidia.

The U.S. has banned the export of advanced AI chips to China since 2022 in an effort to limit its advancement. Even though President Trump eventually allowed Nvidia to export H100 AI GPUs to China in December 2025, companies that wanted to buy them needed to secure an import license from the Commerce Department. Aside from that, the White House is also collecting a 25% fee for every chip, making it more expensive for a data center in China to buy them. Because of this, many Chinese AI labs are instead renting compute from data centers located abroad. They mostly do this in Southeast Asia, which is just around a four-to-six-hour flight from major cities like Hong Kong, Shenzhen, and Shanghai, although some firms are looking in further areas like Europe.

It remains unclear if Nvidia’s order is permanent or temporary, but the new export rules give cloud providers 90 days to apply for an export license and prove that their customers do not include restricted entities. However, one cloud provider executive told the Financial Times that there are no clear instructions on how to go about this. “There’s this new rule but no guidance on execution,” they said. “No one knows how to apply for this license while the 90-day clock is ticking. It’s causing sheer chaos in the region.”

This sudden halt of orders is causing trouble for many data centers in Southeast Asia. Sources say that Aolani, a Singapore-based cloud company with a deal to provide compute to ByteDance, is losing financing for the project as bankers have stopped issuing credit to the tune of $500 million as Nvidia GPU shipments to the region remain in jeopardy. The Chinese social media giant could also potentially drop out of the arrangement, as it won’t be able to access the powerful Nvidia chips that many AI labs still prefer for training their models.

#10849. Scientists are seriously asking if bees and ChatGPT are conscious

You might think a honey bee foraging in your garden and a browser window running ChatGPT have nothing in common. But recent scientific research has been seriously considering the possibility that either, or both, might be conscious.

There are many different ways of studying consciousness. One of the most common is to measure how an animal – or artificial intelligence (AI) – acts.

But two new papers on the possibility of consciousness in animals and AI suggest new theories for how to test this – one that strikes a middle ground between sensationalism and knee-jerk skepticism about whether humans are the only conscious beings on Earth.

Questions around consciousness have long sparked fierce debate.

That’s in part because conscious beings might matter morally in a way that unconscious things don’t. Expanding the sphere of consciousness means expanding our ethical horizons. Even if we can’t be sure something is conscious, we might err on the side of caution by assuming it is – what philosopher Jonathan Birch calls the precautionary principle for sentience.

The recent trend has been one of expansion.

For example, in April 2024 a group of 40 scientists at a conference in New York proposed the New York Declaration on Animal Consciousness. Subsequently signed by over 500 scientists and philosophers, this declaration says consciousness is realistically possible in all vertebrates (including reptiles, amphibians and fishes) as well as many invertebrates, including cephalopods (octopus and squid), crustaceans (crabs and lobsters) and insects.

In parallel with this, the incredible rise of large language models, such as ChatGPT, has raised the serious possibility that machines may be conscious.

Five years ago, a seemingly ironclad test of whether something was conscious was to see if you could have a conversation with it. Philosopher Susan Schneider suggested if we had an AI that convincingly mused on the metaphysics of consciousness, it may well be conscious.

By those standards, today we would be surrounded by conscious machines. Many have gone so far as to apply the precautionary principle here too: the burgeoning field of AI welfare is devoted to figuring out if and when we must care about machines.

Yet all of these arguments depend, in large part, on surface-level behavior. But that behavior can be deceptive. What matters for consciousness is not what you do, but how you do it.

A new paper in Trends in Cognitive Sciences that one of us (Colin Klein) coauthored, drawing on previous work, looks to the machinery rather than the behavior of AI.

It also draws on the cognitive science tradition to identify a plausible list of indicators of consciousness based on the structure of information processing. This means one can draw up a useful list of indicators of consciousness without having to agree on which of the current cognitive theories of consciousness is correct.

Some indicators (such as the need to resolve trade-offs between competing goals in contextually appropriate ways) are shared by many theories. Most other indicators (such as the presence of informational feedback) are only required by one theory but indicative in others.

Importantly, the useful indicators are all structural. They all have to do with how brains and computers process and combine information.

The verdict? No existing AI system (including ChatGPT) is conscious. The appearance of consciousness in large language models is not achieved in a way that is sufficiently similar to us to warrant attribution of conscious states.

Yet at the same time, there is no bar to AI systems – perhaps ones with a very different architecture to today’s systems – becoming conscious.

The lesson? It’s possible for AI to behave as if conscious without being conscious.

Biologists are also turning to mechanisms – how brains work – to recognize consciousness in non-human animals.

In a new paper in Philosophical Transactions B, we propose a neural model for minimal consciousness in insects. This is a model that abstracts away from anatomical detail to focus on the core computations done by simple brains.

Our key insight is to identify the kind of computation our brains perform that gives rise to experience.

This computation solves ancient problems from our evolutionary history that arise from having a mobile, complex body with many senses and conflicting needs.

Importantly, we don’t identify the computation itself – there is science yet to be done. But we show that if you could identify it, you’d have a level playing field to compare humans, invertebrates, and computers.

The problem of consciousness in animals and in computers appear to pull in different directions.

For animals, the question is often how to interpret whether ambiguous behavior (like a crab tending its wounds) indicates consciousness.

For computers, we have to decide whether apparently unambiguous behavior (a chatbot musing with you on the purpose of existence) is a true indicator of consciousness or mere roleplay.

Yet as the fields of neuroscience and AI progress, both are converging on the same lesson: when making judgement about whether something is consciousness, how it works is proving more informative than what it does.

Materials provided by The Conversation. Original written by Colin Klein and Andrew Barron. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10845. A classic brain test exposed AI's biggest weakness

Artificial intelligence systems can write essays, answer questions, and solve complex problems. But new research suggests they may struggle with something humans do every day: staying focused on the task at hand when distractions get in the way.

Researchers led by Suketu Patel put several leading AI models through a well-known psychology experiment called the Stroop task. The results revealed a significant difference between how AI systems process information and how the human brain manages attention.

The Stroop task is a classic psychological test that has been used for decades to study attention, concentration, and self-control.

In the test, color words such as "red," "blue," or "green" are displayed in colored ink. Sometimes the word and the ink color match. For example, the word "red" might appear in red ink. Other times they conflict, such as the word "red" printed in blue ink.

Participants are asked to name the color of the ink rather than read the word itself.

That sounds simple, but it creates a challenge because reading words is an automatic habit for most people. The brain must suppress the urge to read the word and instead focus on identifying the ink color.

Psychologists often use the task to measure what is known as executive control, a set of mental processes that helps people regulate attention, resist distractions, and stay focused on goals.

The researchers wanted to see whether modern large language models (LLMs) handle this challenge in the same way humans do.

LLMs are the AI systems behind tools such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini. They are trained on enormous amounts of text and learn patterns in language, allowing them to generate responses that often appear remarkably human.

When given short lists containing five color words, the AI systems generally performed well, even when the words and colors did not match.

However, the picture changed dramatically as the lists became longer.

GPT-4o achieved 91% accuracy when working with five words. At ten words, its accuracy fell to 57%. When the list expanded to forty words, accuracy dropped to just 15%.

Claude 3.5 Sonnet maintained stable performance through lists of twenty words but then experienced a sharp decline, falling to 24% accuracy with forty-word lists.

The researchers observed similar patterns in GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1, and Gemini 2.5.

The challenge became even more difficult when matching and mismatched color words appeared together in the same list.

Under those conditions, performance deteriorated further. Accuracy for the mismatched items dropped to nearly zero in some cases.

According to the researchers, the AI models had trouble maintaining the instruction to identify ink colors. Instead, they increasingly defaulted to reading the words themselves.

In other words, the systems appeared unable to consistently suppress the response they had been most heavily trained to produce.

This finding is particularly interesting because humans face a similar conflict. People are generally much better at reading words than naming ink colors. Yet despite this bias, most individuals can maintain high accuracy and stable performance even when confronted with long lists of conflicting words and colors.

The study highlights an important distinction between human and artificial intelligence.

Although modern AI systems can produce impressive language and reasoning capabilities, their underlying mechanisms differ from the attention processes found in biological brains.

Humans can often sustain focus on a specific goal while filtering out competing information. The results suggest that current AI models may struggle with this type of cognitive control when tasks become increasingly demanding.

The researchers argue that the performance collapse seen in these experiments points to fundamental limitations in today's large language models. While AI can sometimes mimic human behavior, its ability to maintain attention appears to operate very differently from the way people do.

The findings offer a reminder that even the most advanced AI systems still have weaknesses, particularly when tasks require them to resist distractions and stay focused over extended sequences of information.

Materials provided by PNAS Nexus. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
