#10843. Data-Driven Pages That Never Go Stale: A Practical Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every developer has shipped the other kind of page: a lovingly written article or landing page that peaks on launch day and then quietly rots. Screenshots age, statistics expire, rankings slide. Data-driven pages play the opposite game. Built from a living dataset, they republish themselves daily, grow to thousands of URLs, and answer questions nobody had asked when the site launched. This guide breaks down how they work, using a real fan-run stats site as the running example: one public dataset in, more than ten thousand fresh pages out.

Static pages lose ground because the web moves and they do not. Facts expire, competitors publish fresher answers, and search engines reward pages whose information matches today. A page that cannot update itself must be rewritten by hand, so most sites update a few pages and abandon the rest.

Freshness is not one rule for the whole web; it depends on the query. Nobody needs a CSS tutorial rewritten weekly, but statistics, prices, availability and anything with a season attached decays on a schedule. Developers misjudge this constantly, treating every page like documentation when half their catalogue is really a perishable good. Serve a three-month-old stat as if it were current and readers learn the most damaging lesson a site can teach: do not trust the numbers here.

Then there is the maintenance maths. A hundred hand-written pages each needing one review a year is a hundred small debts, and the interest compounds as the catalogue grows. The team updates the flagship pages, promises to return for the rest, and quietly never does. Data-driven architecture is really a staffing decision disguised as a technical one: humans design the system once, and the system does the updating forever.

A data-driven site has three layers: a dataset that refreshes on a schedule, templates that turn records into pages, and URL patterns that map every meaningful combination users might search for. Change the data and every page changes; improve a template and ten thousand pages improve at once.

The running example makes this concrete. A fan-built Overwatch stats tracker republishes Blizzard's public hero data on a daily cycle. From that one feed it generates hero profiles, head-to-head matchup pages and multi-condition tier lists: 10,291 URLs in its sitemap, across six languages, with matchup pages alone accounting for 8,586 of them. Nobody wrote those pages. Somebody designed the system that writes them, which is a very different, and much more scalable, job.

The example runs on Next.js behind a CDN, but the stack is the least important layer of the three. The same pipeline feeding WordPress, Astro or a static site generator would look identical from the reader's side of the glass. Choose boring technology you already know, and spend your originality budget on the dataset and the templates, because that is where readers will feel the difference.

Generated pages stay useful when every URL must earn its existence with data. Set minimum thresholds before a page is published, give each template unique modules its siblings do not have, and noindex combinations with nothing to say. The line between a resource and spam is not automation; it is emptiness.

Quality gates work best when they are public and mechanical. The example site publishes its tier formula openly: 70% of the score from win rate, 30% from pick rate, and a hard floor that excludes heroes picked in under 0.5% of matches from topping the ranking, because a tiny sample cannot prove anything. That single floor is editorial policy expressed as code. Your version might be a minimum number of data points, a minimum price history, or a minimum review count — the number matters less than the fact that the gate exists and applies to every page equally.

Each template also needs modules its siblings cannot copy. A hero profile carries a seven-day trend and its best and worst matchups; a matchup page carries the head-to-head history; a tier list carries the movement since yesterday. Add a short human layer on top — a methodology page explaining the scoring, changelogs narrating what moved — and the catalogue stops reading as output and starts reading as a publication. Google's scaled-content rules target pages manufactured to manipulate rankings with little value; a page that answers a real query with real, current data is the opposite of that. The honest test for any generated URL is simple: if it vanished tonight, would a searcher lose an answer?

Start embarrassingly small: one dataset, one entity type, one template. A football club's results archive, a property portal's sold prices, your own product catalogue — anything structured and refreshed beats a grand plan. Prove one template earns traffic, then add combinations, languages and dashboards in that order.

What is programmatic SEO? Programmatic SEO means generating many pages from a structured dataset using templates, instead of writing each page by hand. Done well, each page answers a distinct search with real data. Done badly, it produces thousands of near-identical pages, which is where its reputation suffers.

Is programmatic SEO against Google's rules? No. Google's spam policies target scaled content created mainly to manipulate rankings, with little or no value for users. Pages generated from real data that answer real queries are publishing. The deciding question is whether a page helps its reader, not how it was produced.

How often should the data behind pages refresh? Match the refresh to how fast the underlying facts change. Sports and game statistics justify daily pulls, prices often need hourly checks, and reference data may only move monthly. Refreshing faster than reality changes wastes crawl budget and makes 'updated' stamps meaningless.

Do generated pages need unique written text? They need unique value, which is not the same thing. Identical paragraphs on ten thousand pages add nothing, but unique data, comparisons and trends are content in their own right. Add short human-written context per template, and let the data carry the differences between pages.

How many pages should a first project launch with? Launch with the smallest set that proves the template: one entity type, a few hundred pages at most. Validate that search engines index them and users engage before adding combinations and languages. Scaling an unproven template just manufactures tomorrow's cleanup job at industrial size.

The sites quietly winning the long game are not publishing more; they are publishing systems. One honest dataset, one well-built template and strict quality gates will outlast a hundred hand-written pages competing on freshness alone. Try it this week: take one dataset you already own, design a single template with four real modules, and ship fifty pages. Watch what the data does next.

Even Elon Musk can't seem to get special treatment.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The memory shortage has gotten so bad that many companies, especially smaller ones, have started establishing “procurement war rooms,” where employees are desperately trying to find memory chip supplies on sites like eBay and trying to figure out how they could make manufacturers offer them even just a small amount of DRAM. This massive shortage has pushed up costs for electronic manufacturers, with some companies forced to increase prices to make up the difference while others cut the specifications of their products while introducing a smaller price bump.

The Wall Street Journal reports that Taiwanese memory module and storage maker Apacer had to take out loans just to have money on hand in case it finds a supply of memory chips. “If chips show up, we take it,” Apacer President Gibson Chen told the publication. “This is crazy, but there is no other way.” Even one of the world’s richest people, Elon Musk, was reportedly having trouble finding memory chips. A portfolio manager claimed that Musk offered Samsung an LTA and even a financial guarantee for new Samsung chip factories, but the company’s executive chairman, Lee Jae-yong, allegedly answered that everyone else was offering those things already. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman also recently went to South Korea to meet with Samsung leadership just to secure more RAM for data centers.

Even though PC builders and enthusiasts were the hardest hit by the 500% climb in memory prices, many other electronics manufacturers are also now feeling the squeeze. Big companies like Amazon, Apple, and Microsoft could use their scale and resources to pay more and secure massive amounts of memory chip supplies under long-term agreements (LTAs). However, smaller firms would usually have to rely on brokers and whatever’s available to find what they need. Some have reportedly contacted hundreds of brokers and go through online listings, where they would snap up even small batches of just five or 12 units just to have some supply. Others proceed to the gray market, although they must be careful as some unscrupulous sellers mix in batches of used memory chips with brand new orders to inflate their deliveries.

The scramble for AI compute has caused a massive memory chip shortage, especially as the big three memory chip manufacturers — Micron, Samsung, and SK hynix — have focused their production on more lucrative HBM. Both memory chip manufacturers and RAM kit makers are warning of continued shortages, too, with SK hynix saying 2027 will be the “worst year” for memory shortages, while TeamGroup’s CEO said that it could reach a point where “even money can’t buy goods.” Although the big three manufacturers are building new factories, they’re not expected to go online and start pushing out memory chips until 2028.

It’s difficult for a new player to enter the memory chip market. Aside from the enormous costs involved in setting up a factory and supply chain to build memory chips, any potential entrant must also license a ton of patents and develop its own technologies before it can start making competitive chips. Even if it was able to get its hands on the knowledge required to design memory chips, it still needs the manufacturing expertise needed to produce them at scale. CXMT is the only new player that has successfully produced mainstream consumer DDR5 RAM, but it’s facing multiple allegations that it stole the technology needed to achieve that from Samsung. Even then, the DDR5 memory chips that the Chinese company produced don’t have the same performance as its competitors and track market prices, meaning it’s not the affordable alternative that many tech enthusiasts imagined it to be.

The memory chip market is known for its boom-and-bust cycle, which is why manufacturers were initially reluctant to increase their capacity. But even if they started building factories at the first sign of a shortage, it would still take several years before these new facilities could start pumping out chips to ease the situation. Nevertheless, the three companies are currently facing a price-fixing lawsuit alleging that they coordinated their shift to HBM to cut DDR3 and DDR4 production, with the judge handling the case saying that it’s plausible they colluded as the market experienced the price surge.

#10839. Brain-inspired chip runs near absolute zero and could transform quantum computing

Researchers at the University of Hong Kong (HKU) have unveiled a significant advance in cryogenic electronics that could help overcome key challenges in quantum computing and support future deep space missions. The team, from HKU's Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering and the Centre for Advanced Semiconductors and Integrated Circuits (CASIC), developed a programmable neuromorphic hardware platform capable of operating at temperatures near absolute zero.

The research was led by Professor Yuhao Zhang and PhD student Xin Yang. Their work introduces a new method for generating and controlling negative differential resistance (NDR) in industry standard Silicon Carbide (SiC) MOSFETs. Using this approach, the researchers demonstrated for the first time that a single transistor can reproduce the energy efficient "spiking" activity of biological neurons at temperatures as low as 10mK.

Brain-Inspired Hardware for Quantum Computing

Quantum computers depend on sophisticated control electronics to manage qubits, which are highly sensitive and must be kept at millikelvin temperatures. Existing silicon based control systems consume considerable power and produce unwanted heat, making it necessary to position them away from the qubits themselves. That distance creates extensive wiring requirements that can hinder performance and make large scale quantum computers more difficult to build.

"Our work introduces a hardware platform that can be integrated alongside quantum processors," said Professor Zhang. "By using the unique carrier dynamics in silicon carbide, we can create circuits that are thousands of times more energy-efficient than conventional electronics, significantly reducing the thermal load on cryogenic systems."

Silicon Carbide Reveals Unique Cryogenic Behavior

The team found that SiC MOSFETs display a strong "S-shape" NDR effect when cooled below 2K. This behavior is driven by electron-donor impact ionization (EDII). Unlike other technologies that depend on heat generated within a device, the newly observed mechanism arises directly from the material's atomic properties. As a result, it remains highly stable and can be reproduced consistently across different manufacturing batches.

"This is a robust and scalable approach," said Mr. Yang. "Because SiC is already used globally in electric vehicles and power grids, we can leverage existing industrial foundries to manufacture these cryogenic chips on 300-mm wafers."

From Artificial Neurons to Deep Space Missions

The study also demonstrated that these artificial neurons can be linked together, or "cascaded," into larger networks. This capability could enable advanced local data processing at cryogenic temperatures and improve important quantum computing functions such as quantum error correction and real time quantum control.

The potential applications extend well beyond quantum computing. Because the circuits are designed to operate reliably in extremely cold environments, they could also be valuable for deep space exploration. Future systems may be able to function in the harsh conditions found on the Moon's surface or in the distant regions of our solar system.

The findings were published in Nature Communications in a paper titled "Cryogenic neuromorphic circuits using gate-controlled negative differential resistance in silicon carbide."

Materials provided by The University of Hong Kong. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10835. SpaceX wants to build AI data centers in space. Will it work?

Imagine if one company could become the railroad, electric utility and cloud-computing provider of the emerging space economy. That potential fueled excitement around the long-anticipated initial public offering of SpaceX. Investors are not simply betting on rockets anymore. They are betting on an entire orbital ecosystem.

Among the most ambitious and challenging ideas riding this wave of enthusiasm is something that sounds almost like science fiction: orbital data centers. SpaceX may be one of the most well-known companies seeking to build them, but it is not the only one.

The logic is seductive: Launch the data centers into orbit, where solar energy is abundant and land, water and local power grids are no longer constraints. As artificial intelligence drives an explosion in computing demand, companies are pitching orbital data centers as a way to escape the growing environmental and infrastructure pressures of Earth-based computing. Data centers often also face backlash from the public at having these centers located in their communities.

But there is a vast difference between launching satellites and operating an industrial-scale computing infrastructure in orbit. Space is unforgiving. Radiation damages electronics. The electronics generate enormous amounts of heat, and getting rid of that heat is surprisingly difficult in space. Repairs are extraordinarily expensive, and every pound launched into orbit still carries a significant cost.

We are engineering professors who study data center design and space systems engineering. Building a space-based data center will involve considerations from both sides.

First off, consider what goes into an Earth-based data center, like those that you’ve probably begun to see pop up everywhere. These facilities power cloud computing, video streaming, online banking, scientific computing and, increasingly, artificial intelligence. But a data center is much more than a room full of servers.

A data center needs several things to operate reliably. The first is electric power. Servers, networking equipment and storage devices consume large amounts of electricity, and that power demand is growing rapidly with AI.

The second is cooling. Almost all the electricity consumed by servers eventually becomes heat. If that heat is not removed quickly and reliably, equipment performance drops, failures increase and the data center can shut down. Cooling systems often include air handling units, chillers, cooling towers, pumps and, increasingly, liquid-cooling equipment. In many facilities, cooling is the largest energy consumer after the computing equipment itself.

The third is physical infrastructure, including the necessary land, buildings, structural support, backup power, water systems, communication networks and maintenance access. Data centers also need to be close enough to users and network backbones to provide fast digital services.

In short, Earth-based data centers are large electrical and thermal infrastructure systems built around computing hardware.

So what would it take to build these data centers in space, and why are companies finding this possibility such an interesting business proposition?

As on Earth, these data centers would require massive amounts of power. In space, this power would come from solar panels. The Sun always shines in space and can’t be blocked by clouds. However, depending on the orbit the solar panels are put in, the Earth may shadow them for some portion of the orbit.

And even the best solar cells available today can convert only about half the sunlight that hits them to electricity.

Another potential advantage found in space is cooling. The cold background of space (near minus 455 degrees Fahrenheit, or minus 270 degrees Celsius) creates an opportunity: waste heat from the data center could escape into space through radiators, keeping the electronics cool.

In principle, that design could eliminate some of the bulky and water-intensive cooling infrastructure used on Earth. However, those thermal radiators would require a large amount of surface area, and that would be in addition to the area required by the solar panels.

In space, there is no air to blow across hot equipment and help heat escape. The heat has to leave as infrared radiation, which is a relatively slow process. As a result, removing 10 megawatts of waste heat can require radiator surfaces comparable to the size of two football fields.

Space-based data centers could also avoid some of the local conflicts that come with building large data centers on the ground. Many communities resist new data center developments because of their land use, energy and water demand, and noise and environmental impact.

A space-based system would avoid competing for local land and water resources, and it would not generate neighborhood noise or require local zoning approval in the same way.

However, space is already getting crowded, and launching thousands of large orbital data centers would accelerate this issue. Orbital debris and micrometeorites are hazards because they can puncture the space data center, and a worst-case collision could destroy it and create even more space debris.

The frequency of space launches necessary to send all the equipment to orbit may also become a concern for some communities. SpaceX has had protests at its launch complex in Boca Chica, Texas, from local activists who argue that its rocket testing and launches damage the surrounding environment.

All that data would need to be sent between Earth and these data centers – and between the data centers themselves – using radio waves or laser communications systems. Although satellite constellations such as Starlink and Amazon Leo have demonstrated that doing this is possible, the amount of data sent to and from space would balloon.

These data centers, along with their solar panels and radiators, cannot be launched in one piece and would need to be assembled in space. This process would require new equipment for in-space servicing, assembly and manufacturing.

Another key challenge is the refresh cycle of computing hardware. Data center servers are not built to last forever. Operators on Earth usually replace or upgrade hardware every three to five years as chips improve, workloads change and equipment ages.

And equipment failures can require replacing components. The refresh and repair processes are relatively straightforward on Earth, where workers can physically remove and replace servers.

In space, refresh and repair becomes much harder. Hardware sent to orbit may be difficult or too expensive to upgrade. If the computing platform cannot be updated, or too many components fail, it may become obsolete long before the surrounding infrastructure reaches the end of its useful life.

In a field where performance improves so rapidly and demand from computing continues to increase, this hurdle could prove a major economic and operational challenge.

Then there is the harshness of space. These data centers would be in a near vacuum, with constant radiation hitting them. And depending on their orbit, they would go from hot when in the sunlight to cold in Earth’s shadow many times a day. All of these challenges, and more, are issues that will need to be addressed.

Despite these challenges, companies are moving forward with designing space-based data centers. SpaceX just announced the design for its AI1 Compute Satellite, which it hopes to use as an orbital data center spacecraft. However, this satellite is 100 to 1,000 times less capable than current Earth-based data centers.

Not every computing task makes sense to do in space. Many data center applications depend on fast response times and close connections to users on Earth. Financial transactions, interactive AI services and most cloud applications are extremely sensitive to delay.

More feasible early applications may be those that are less latency-sensitive and more tightly connected to space operations. Examples could include processing Earth observation data from satellites, military or intelligence data processing, scientific computing related to space missions, or specialized computing for satellites and other space assets.

In other words, the first viable space data centers may serve space-based customers before they compete with mainstream cloud data centers on Earth.

#10834. Running WireGuard for a Development Team: The Hidden Cost of VPN Maintenance

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Setting up WireGuard on a small virtual server is a satisfying infrastructure task. Install the package, generate a key pair, open a UDP port, configure a peer, and connect a laptop. Within an afternoon, your staging environment can sit behind a private tunnel, and your internal dashboard can be restricted to traffic from a known address.

The initial setup is rarely the difficult part.

The real engineering work starts when the team changes. A developer joins, another leaves, someone replaces a laptop, a server needs patching, or a connection suddenly stops working. Each event creates a small maintenance task. Over time, those tasks become an operational responsibility that somebody must own.

This article examines what it takes to operate a self-hosted WireGuard VPN for a development team. We'll look at peer configuration, access management, troubleshooting, monitoring tools, and the engineering time involved. We'll also compare self-hosting with managed business VPN services so teams can make a decision based on their infrastructure needs.

WireGuard is a modern VPN protocol designed to establish encrypted connections between network peers. Its relatively small codebase, modern cryptography, and straightforward configuration make it attractive for developers who want control over their network infrastructure.

A basic WireGuard deployment typically involves:

A server with a reachable public IP address.

A private and public key pair for the server.

A UDP port through which peers communicate.

Peer configuration that defines which addresses are allowed through the tunnel.

A simplified server configuration might look like this:

The corresponding client configuration contains the client's private key and the server's public key:

These examples illustrate the basic relationship between a server and a client. A production configuration will also need appropriate firewall rules, routing, DNS settings, and a plan for distributing configuration securely.

The important distinction is that WireGuard establishes encrypted peer connections. It does not automatically provide a complete employee identity system, onboarding workflow, approval process, or audit dashboard.

Those responsibilities belong to the team operating the infrastructure.

Developers interested in using WireGuard on personal development devices can also explore SitePoint's guide to building a hardened mobile development environment with Termux, Docker, and WireGuard.

A peer is not just an entry in a configuration file. It represents a device that has permission to communicate through your network.

That makes peer management an access-control responsibility.

For a small development team, it is tempting to keep a single configuration file and update it manually whenever someone joins or leaves. This can work initially, but it becomes harder to manage as the number of devices increases.

A useful first step is to maintain a peer inventory:

The inventory should not contain private keys. Private keys must remain on their respective devices and should never be placed in a shared spreadsheet or source repository.

The inventory instead helps administrators answer practical questions:

Which device is associated with this tunnel address?

Has the peer been removed after the device was lost or replaced?

This information becomes especially important when a team uses the VPN to reach staging servers, internal dashboards, databases, or other development resources.

Manual onboarding is manageable when a team has only two or three developers. It becomes unreliable when several people join, leave, or change devices over the year.

A repeatable onboarding process should cover more than generating a key pair.

Confirm that the team member is authorized to access the environment.

Generate a key pair on the user's device.

Add the public key to the server's peer configuration.

Apply the appropriate routing and firewall permissions.

Deliver the client configuration through a secure channel.

Test access to the specific development resources the person needs.

Record the peer owner, device, and approval details.

Offboarding needs an equally clear process:

Identify every peer associated with the departing team member.

Remove the relevant public keys from the server.

Confirm that the configuration change has been applied.

Review any other credentials or allowlists that granted access.

An administrator can inspect the currently configured peers with:

The output helps identify peer public keys, transfer counters, and latest handshake information. It is useful for troubleshooting, but it should not be treated as a complete access audit or proof that a particular person is still authorized.

The broader lesson is to make access changes part of a documented workflow. A peer should not remain active simply because nobody remembers who created it.

One common network-design mistake is assuming that connecting to a VPN should give a developer access to every internal service.

A developer working on a frontend application may need access to a staging web server but not to the production database. A contractor may need access to one test environment without being able to reach internal administration tools.

WireGuard's peer configuration and the surrounding network controls should be designed around those requirements.

Separate tunnel address ranges for different groups.

Firewall rules that restrict traffic to approved services.

Cloud security groups that limit access to specific ports.

Application-level authentication for internal dashboards.

Separate production and staging network boundaries.

The exact implementation depends on the infrastructure. The key point is that VPN membership and application authorization are different controls.

A successful VPN handshake should not be treated as permission to access every resource behind the tunnel.

A VPN can appear simple when it works. Diagnosing it becomes more complicated when a developer is connecting from a home router, a hotel network, or a mobile hotspot.

Incorrect routing or AllowedIPs settings.

A server process or host becoming unavailable.

WireGuard provides useful inspection commands:

These commands help inspect peer information, check the service state, and review recent service logs. The exact systemd service name can differ depending on how the interface was configured.

Administrators should also monitor the underlying server. At minimum, that means checking:

Whether the WireGuard interface is active.

Whether disk or memory usage is approaching a limit.

Whether operating-system and package updates are pending.

A small virtual server may be inexpensive, but it is still infrastructure that needs an owner.

Teams working with alternative tunnel-based development workflows may find SitePoint's tutorial on setting up Cloudflare Tunnels with Docker Compose for persistent webhook debugging useful. It covers a different tunneling use case, but the operational questions around connectivity, service health, and troubleshooting are closely related.

A self-hosted VPN does not need to become a collection of manual tasks. A few established tools can make configuration, monitoring, and debugging more repeatable.

The core tool is, of course, WireGuard. Its command-line utilities allow administrators to inspect peers, manage interfaces, and review connection information. The official documentation is the best starting point for understanding configuration options and platform support.

Ansible can automate repeatable server administration tasks. A team could use playbooks to deploy configuration files, apply system updates, or standardize firewall settings across several VPN hosts.

Sensitive values should be handled with an appropriate secrets-management approach. Private keys should not be committed to a Git repository or stored in plaintext playbooks.

Prometheus is useful for collecting and querying metrics, while Node Exporter exposes Linux host metrics such as CPU, memory, disk, and network statistics.

These tools do not automatically provide a complete WireGuard security audit. They can, however, help identify resource problems and server-health issues before those problems become user-reported outages.

Uptime Kuma provides a self-hosted monitoring dashboard with notifications. It can help teams monitor the availability of a VPN host or related services.

A basic availability check does not prove that every VPN peer can connect successfully. For more meaningful monitoring, combine host checks with an end-to-end test from a separate network.

Wireshark and tcpdump help developers inspect network traffic when diagnosing connectivity problems.

For example, tcpdump can capture UDP traffic on the WireGuard port:

This can help determine whether packets are reaching the server. It does not decrypt WireGuard traffic or replace checks of routing, firewall rules, and peer configuration.

The right toolset depends on the size of the deployment. A team with one VPN server may only need WireGuard's own commands and basic host monitoring. A larger environment may benefit from automation, centralized metrics, and a documented incident process.

A self-hosted VPN can cost very little in direct infrastructure charges. However, the server invoice does not include the engineering time required to operate it.

Consider a hypothetical team with these assumptions:

Two people join and two leave during the year.

The team reviews or rotates device keys quarterly.

Patching and monitoring require about two hours per month.

Six troubleshooting incidents occur annually.

The estimated loaded engineering cost is $60 per hour.

The virtual server costs approximately $6 per month.

The following estimate illustrates how quickly maintenance time can exceed the infrastructure bill.

At $60 per hour, 46 hours represent approximately $2,760 in engineering time. The server adds around $72 per year, bringing the estimated total to $2,832.

These figures are illustrative, not a universal cost benchmark. A team with infrastructure automation and established monitoring may spend considerably less time. A team with frequent staff changes or strict audit requirements may spend more.

The useful exercise is to measure the work your own team performs. Track onboarding, access reviews, patching, incidents, and recovery time for a few months. That gives you a more realistic comparison than looking at hosting prices alone.

Self-hosting is not automatically the best option just because a team has developers who can configure servers.

A managed business VPN may be worth considering when the team needs centralized member management, simpler device administration, or a stable network exit address without maintaining the underlying VPN infrastructure.

Depending on the provider and plan, managed services may offer:

A central dashboard for adding and removing users.

Client applications for common operating systems.

Multi-factor authentication or identity integrations.

For example, PureVPN for Teams is one service in this category. Teams evaluating it—or any other provider—should check the current feature set, pricing, logging practices, access controls, and service terms before making a decision.

A managed service does not remove every security responsibility. The team still needs to decide who receives access, review permissions, protect user accounts, and understand how traffic and metadata are handled.

It also introduces vendor dependency and a recurring subscription cost. Those trade-offs should be considered alongside the engineering time saved.

There are situations where running WireGuard yourself is the more practical option.

You already operate infrastructure. If your team routinely patches servers, manages secrets, monitors services, and responds to incidents, maintaining a VPN may add relatively little work.

You need custom routing. A deployment that connects several private cloud networks, on-premises environments, or specialized subnets may require network controls that are easier to implement directly.

You have strict data-control requirements. Some organizations have policies that limit which third parties can handle network traffic or related metadata. Those requirements may favor infrastructure under the organization's own control.

You have a dedicated platform or security team. If someone is explicitly responsible for access management, monitoring, and incident response, self-hosting can fit naturally into existing operations.

You can automate the lifecycle. Infrastructure-as-code, configuration management, documented peer inventories, and tested recovery procedures can reduce repetitive work and make a self-hosted deployment easier to maintain.

The important distinction is between a deliberately operated service and a one-off server that nobody owns.

Before choosing an approach, ask the following questions:

Who is responsible for the VPN six or twelve months after deployment?

How many people and devices are likely to join or leave?

How are public keys and peer configurations tracked?

How quickly can access be revoked when a device is lost?

Which internal services should each developer be able to reach?

Do you need a fixed egress IP for external allowlists?

What monitoring and recovery process exists if the server fails?

Do your customers or internal policies require access logs or formal reviews?

Does your team have the time and expertise to maintain the infrastructure?

Would a managed service cost less than the engineering time and operational risk it replaces?

If your team already has mature infrastructure practices and needs custom network control, self-hosting may be a sensible choice.

If the team is small, access changes frequently, and nobody has time to own another server, a managed service may be more practical.

WireGuard is an excellent protocol, and deploying it can be a straightforward engineering task. But operating a VPN for a development team involves more than establishing an encrypted connection.

Peer management, onboarding, offboarding, network permissions, monitoring, patching, and troubleshooting all require ongoing attention.

The right choice depends on the team's infrastructure experience, security requirements, size, and available engineering time. Self-hosting offers control and flexibility; a managed service can reduce operational work. Neither option removes the need for sound access-control practices.

Before deciding, calculate the maintenance effort, identify who will own the service, and make sure the design can be maintained as the team changes.

The cost example uses illustrative assumptions rather than current vendor pricing. Managed VPN features and prices can change, so verify provider details before purchasing.

#10829. Millions of exploding stars could soon reveal dark energy's secrets

Researchers led by the Institute of Cosmos Sciences of the University of Barcelona (ICCUB) have developed a new technique that could greatly improve how scientists study the expansion of the Universe and investigate the mysterious force known as dark energy.

Published in Nature Astronomy, the research introduces a framework called CIGaRS that can extract far more information from Type Ia supernovae, the powerful stellar explosions used to measure vast cosmic distances. Unlike many current approaches, the method relies mainly on imaging data rather than expensive spectroscopic observations. The advance is expected to help astronomers take full advantage of the enormous datasets that will soon arrive from next generation sky surveys, particularly those conducted by the Vera C. Rubin Observatory.

Type Ia supernovae occur when white dwarf stars explode. Because these explosions reach nearly the same intrinsic brightness, astronomers use them as "standard candles": by comparing their actual brightness with how bright they appear from Earth, researchers can calculate their distance.

These measurements played a crucial role in the discovery that the Universe is expanding at an accelerating rate. Scientists attribute that acceleration to dark energy, one of the most significant unsolved questions in modern physics.

However, there is an important complication. Type Ia supernovae are not perfectly identical.

How Host Galaxies Affect Supernova Measurements

Over the past 20 years, astronomers have found that a supernova's observed brightness is influenced by the galaxy where it occurs. Supernovae found in older or more massive galaxies can appear slightly different from those occurring in younger or less massive galaxies.

Researchers have typically accounted for these differences using relatively simple correction methods. While useful, these approximations can limit the accuracy of distance measurements and, in turn, the precision of cosmological studies.

A Unified Model of Supernovae and the Universe

The new framework addresses this challenge by modeling multiple factors simultaneously. Rather than treating each component independently, the researchers built a single, integrated model that includes the supernova explosions themselves, their host galaxies, the dust that alters their light, changes in supernova rates throughout cosmic history, and even the expansion of the Universe.

By connecting all of these ingredients within one statistical and physical framework, the team can capture relationships that are often overlooked when the pieces are analyzed separately.

"A powerful way of modelling the Universe is to simulate it ab initio in the computer using Bayesian inference," says Raúl Jiménez (ICREA-ICCUB), co-author of the study. "This provides a way to vary all possible parameters at the same time to predict what Universe we live in. Furthermore, by having this capacity, one can look into possible 'unknown unknown' systematics to understand their effect. The impact of these systematics in our inference is arguably the most important missing ingredient in current approaches to model the Universe."

Using Artificial Intelligence To Analyze the Cosmos

Building such a comprehensive model would normally require enormous computing power. To make the approach practical, the researchers turned to a modern technique called simulation-based inference.

The process begins with scientists generating large numbers of simulated universes based on physical models. A neural network (a type of artificial intelligence) then learns how the simulated observations relate to the physical properties that produced them. Once trained, the system can compare real astronomical observations with its simulations and determine the most likely underlying parameters.

This strategy makes it possible to analyze tens of thousands of supernovae simultaneously, a task that would be impractical using traditional techniques.

Accurate Galaxy Distances From Images Alone

One of the study's most significant findings is that the framework can determine galaxy distances (redshifts) with high accuracy using only imaging data.

Redshift measures how much a galaxy's light has been stretched as the Universe expands. It provides information about both the galaxy's distance and how far back in time we are observing it.

According to the researchers, the new method delivers redshift estimates with precision comparable to spectroscopic measurements, but without requiring spectra. This capability is especially important because upcoming surveys are expected to identify millions of supernova candidates, while only a small percentage can realistically receive spectroscopic follow-up observations.

Ready for the Rubin Observatory Data Deluge

The Vera C. Rubin Observatory, currently being built in Chile, is expected to begin a decade long survey of the sky in the near future. During that mission, it will discover an unprecedented number of supernovae. Roughly 99% of those objects will be observed only photometrically, meaning through images taken in different colors rather than detailed spectra.

The CIGaRS framework was specifically developed with this challenge in mind.

"Unlike other frameworks, which require analytic simplifications, our no-compromise end-to-end simulation-based inference approach is uniquely capable of extracting the full cosmological and astrophysical information from the Rubin Observatory's hard-earned data, while avoiding the pitfalls of selection and modelling biases," says Konstantin Karchev (ICCUB-SISSA Trieste), lead author of the study.

The benefits extend beyond measuring dark energy. The framework also provides new information about the origins of Type Ia supernovae themselves.

By reconstructing how supernova occurrence rates vary with the ages of stars in different galaxies, the model helps scientists investigate long standing questions about the systems that eventually produce these explosions.

The researchers found that combining physics based simulations with artificial intelligence can overcome several limitations of current cosmological methods. They estimate that the approach could improve cosmological constraints by as much as a factor of four compared with traditional techniques that depend only on a relatively small sample of spectroscopically observed supernovae.

As the Rubin Observatory prepares to usher in a new era of astronomical discovery, tools such as CIGaRS could help scientists extract the maximum amount of information from its observations and gain a deeper understanding of the Universe.

Materials provided by University of Barcelona. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10827. Football ref removed from match denies being “drunk” after giving yellow card to goalkeeper nowhere near foul

A football referee has denied accusations of being drunk after being removed from a match at halftime following a bizarre incident in which he showed a yellow card to a goalkeeper nearly half the pitch away from a foul.

Syrian referee Hanna Hattab was officiating an AFC Champions League Two match between the United Arab Emirates’ Al Jazira and Iran’s Gol Gohar when his decision-making raised eyebrows.

In the 32nd minute, Hattab awarded a free kick following a foul by Al Jazira’s Felicio Milson and approached the winger with a yellow card in hand.

However, as reported by the Daily Mail, the referee appeared to receive a message through his earpiece before suddenly changing direction and walking toward Al Jazira goalkeeper Ersin Destanoglu.

Despite being nowhere near the foul, the stunned goalkeeper was shown a yellow card.

Hattab eventually overturned the decision after consulting his fourth official, while Milson avoided receiving what would have been his second yellow card of the match.

Gol Gohar manager Mehdi Rahmati was reportedly seen shouting “he’s drunk” at Hattab, while the club’s sporting director, Heydar Afsari, revealed the team had threatened to boycott the second half.

“The referee against Al Jazira was not in a normal state, and even Al Jazira’s players told us the same thing,” Afsari said.

🚨| WATCH: A referee was allegedly DRUNK during an AFC Champions League II match and had to be replaced at half-time. 😳Fans say he looked completely LOST and even booked a goalkeeper for a foul in midfield. Players from both teams say they saw him drinking before kick-off. 😬…

Hattab was ultimately replaced by the fourth official at halftime, with Al Jazira winning 1-0 thanks to a goal from Anderson Talisca.

However, the referee has since denied the allegations and threatened legal action against those responsible for spreading them.

According to BBC Sport, Omani sports journalist Issa Said claimed there was a medical explanation for Hattab’s unusual behavior.

“What happened to him was due to a drop in his blood sugar, which caused him to lose concentration and focus,” he explained.

“He underwent a medical examination after the match and was not intoxicated, contrary to what has been circulating.”

The Syrian Football Association has also backed Hattab, describing the allegations as “entirely unfounded” following an assessment of his health.

The association has requested medical and match officials’ reports from the AFC as part of its investigation, while Hattab has vowed to release a statement detailing exactly what happened.

#10824. The Design Workflow Is Broken. Here Is How Vitalii Khomenko Thinks It Should Be Fixed

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

With 250+ client engagements across both agency and embedded in-house roles, Vitalii Khomenko has seen product teams from the inside in a way few designers have. Here he makes the case for a new kind of design workflow built for the AI era, and explains why the window to adapt is narrower than most founders think.

For the last decade, every startup conversation about speed eventually became a conversation about engineering. Hiring more developers, cutting scope, choosing a simpler stack, it was almost always a dev-side problem. Vitalii Khomenko, Principal Designer at Orizon Design and founder of Fluente LLC, argues that's no longer true. And he has a specific view on what teams should do instead.

What makes Khomenko's perspective worth taking seriously isn't just seniority. It's the specific shape of his experience. Over a career spanning 250+ client engagements, he has worked both as an external specialist brought in to deliver and as an embedded designer who became the single connective thread between product, brand, and execution. That dual vantage point is rare. Most designers live on one side of that line. Khomenko has operated extensively on both, which gives him an unusually clear read on where friction actually originates inside product teams, and where it doesn't.

His work spans early-stage startups finding their first visual language and established brands managing complex, multi-disciplinary design systems. Across that range, certain patterns repeat. And the pattern that started becoming impossible to ignore this year pointed him toward a conclusion that is reshaping how he thinks a modern design workflow should actually be structured.

The bottleneck didn't disappear. It moved. And the workflow needs to move with it.

When shipping code was slow, the existing design workflow made sense. Multiple rounds of revision, user testing, iteration, all of that fit naturally into the window while engineering caught up. That window is now gone, and most design workflows were never updated to reflect it.

"Founders are asking design teams to move at the same speed as an AI-assisted dev pipeline, and design doesn't compress the same way code does," Khomenko says. The result is a workflow that was built around a constraint that no longer exists, applied to a pipeline that has fundamentally changed around it.

The forced choice between polish and speed that product teams are now navigating isn't new as a concept. What's new is how stark it has become, and how directly it traces back to a workflow problem rather than a people problem. "Because development is cheap and fast now, there's constant pressure to ship the next thing immediately," he explains. "But the products that actually retain users are still the ones where the details were considered carefully. Fewer revision rounds isn't a process improvement if it means shipping avoidable friction."

Khomenko has watched this play out across enough teams, at enough stages, to be specific about where the existing workflow breaks down. The issue isn't design judgment. It's where that judgment sits in the process, and what format it's expected to produce at the end.

To understand Khomenko's proposed fix, it helps to understand exactly where the current workflow stalls. For most product teams, the end of the design process looks like this: a designer produces polished, dev-ready screens inside Figma, and hands that file to engineering. Engineering then interprets those screens, translates the visual decisions into code, and builds from there.

That translation step, Figma file to working code, is where the pipeline now breaks down. AI-assisted engineering has made the building part dramatically faster. But the translation step hasn't changed. An engineer still has to read a Figma file, interpret spacing, extract assets, make judgment calls about component structure, and write the implementation from scratch. That process takes the same amount of time it always did.

"The manual workflow inside Figma simply isn't keeping up with AI-boosted engineering," Khomenko says. "The design handoff is quietly becoming the thing everyone is waiting on."

The result is a pipeline where everything around the handoff has accelerated, and the handoff itself has become the single point of drag. Figma, in other words, was built for a world where engineering was the slow part. It wasn't designed to feed a pipeline where engineering moves at AI speed.

This is the core of what Khomenko is proposing, and it's a more significant structural shift than it might first appear.

In his workflow, Figma doesn't disappear. It moves. Rather than serving as both the exploration environment and the final delivery artifact, Figma becomes the visual exploration layer exclusively: the place where brand identity is established, layout logic is worked through, and design direction is validated. It remains the right tool for figuring out what something should look and feel like.

But the final delivery, the artifact that actually goes to engineering, is code. Specifically, components built into prepared libraries that engineering can work from and scale directly, without any translation step in between.

The difference in implementation speed is the point. When a designer delivers a coded component library rather than a Figma file, engineering doesn't need to interpret anything. The decisions are already expressed in the language they're building in. What previously required an engineer to read, interpret, and re-create from a visual reference now arrives ready to use. In a pipeline running on AI-assisted development, that difference compounds fast.

"Figma is still the right place to explore what something should look and feel like," Khomenko says. "But handing over a Figma file and asking engineering to implement it is now the slow path. Delivering in code is the fast path. That's the shift."

Executing this workflow requires a different kind of designer, and Khomenko is direct about that. The UI/UX designer becoming a Design Engineer is, in his assessment, not a distant possibility. It is a matter of time, and the teams treating it as a future consideration rather than a present reality are the ones most likely to find themselves stuck at the handoff.

But Khomenko is equally clear about what this shift doesn't mean. Delivering in code is one part of the role, not the whole of it. The full design cycle isn't going anywhere. Applying branding to imagery, producing animations, building marketing materials, these remain essential creative tasks that require a broad and varied toolkit well beyond code. "The designer of the near future isn't just generating code," he says. "They're moving across the full cycle, brand, motion, marketing, product, with design engineering as an important and growing part of that stack, not a replacement for it."

What changes is that code-ready delivery becomes a core expectation within that stack. The designer who can move fluidly from visual exploration in Figma to component delivery in code, while also carrying the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience, is the profile that the new workflow demands.

Khomenko is candid that no single version of this workflow has fully standardized yet. Every team is figuring it out differently. The tools are evolving, the handoff expectations are shifting, and the processes teams are building around all of it vary considerably. There is no off-the-shelf playbook.

But this is precisely where his 250+ engagements become more than a credential. They're a dataset. Having watched enough teams, at enough stages, through enough cycles of tooling change, Khomenko can see through the noise of individual variation and identify what's directionally true even when the specifics are still in flux. "Each team's workflow is really custom at this stage," he says. "But some general patterns of how this is going to look in the near future are already visible."

Those patterns consistently point in the same direction: design judgment moving earlier, final delivery moving into code, and the designer's role expanding in technical range while staying broad in creative scope. The exact tools and sequences will keep shifting. The underlying logic of the workflow Khomenko is describing is already taking shape in the teams paying closest attention.

For founders and product leads, the workflow Khomenko is describing has direct implications for how teams are staffed, budgeted, and structured.

Front-load design judgment. The biggest leverage in the new workflow isn't faster execution, it's better problem definition before generation starts. Bring senior design thinking in earlier, when it can shape what gets built rather than polish what's already done.

Separate exploration from delivery. Figma and the visual exploration phase serve a distinct purpose from the final delivery layer. Treat them as separate stages with separate expectations, rather than collapsing them into a single step expected to produce everything.

Shift the delivery expectation to code. Whether that means hiring for Design Engineer range, developing it internally, or restructuring handoffs, the end of the design pipeline needs to arrive in a form engineering can work from directly. The translation step is where time goes to die, and it's now the most avoidable part of the process.

Protect the full creative stack. Design engineering matters more than it ever has. It is not, however, the whole job. Teams that reduce design to code generation will lose the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience.

Stay flexible. The workflow is still forming. Teams that build rigidity into their process now will find themselves redoing that work within a year. The teams getting this right are the ones building enough adaptability into their structures to absorb what's still coming.

"The Figma handoff made sense when engineering was the slow part," Khomenko says. "Engineering isn't the slow part anymore. Delivering in code isn't the future of design workflow. For the teams paying attention, it's already the present."

I write about AI and software development from the angle that usually gets skipped: what a spec sheet or benchmark actually implies once you try to build with it. Find me on LinkedIn.

#10822. You can now buy the real-life version of Jason’s GTA 6 house

The real-life version of Jason’s Key Lento home in Grand Theft Auto 6 has gone up for sale, but it’s going to cost you a pretty penny.

After 13 years of waiting, Grand Theft Auto fans were finally able to jump into GTA 6 on November 19. While a few people have completed the game already, others are still making their way through Chapter 1.

The early part of the game has some pretty fun and very South Florida vibes to it, seeing as you’re set in Key Lento, working for Brian Heder. When you’re not doing jobs, you can visit the Rusty Anchor, go on some treasure hunts, or just kick back and relax in Jason’s waterfront house before getting a workout in.

Well, if you’ve got nearly $750,000 to your name, and fancy a move to Florida, then you can now buy a house that has a striking resemblance to Jason’s home.

That’s right, the home is based in Key Largo, Florida, and has been advertised by realtor Jordan Moc.

Unlike Jason’s house, this one actually has two bedrooms – instead of one – but it is on stilts and painted blue. That leaky roof on Jason’s house is nowhere to be seen here, thankfully, and there are concrete steps instead of wooden ones.

Funnily enough, it also has a boat in the yard – just like Jason’s home – and you do have access to the water as well.

The listing has been pounced on by Grand Theft Auto fans, with many asking if they can pay through BuckMe – the game’s banking service.

“Hey, I forgot something under the floorboards, I’ll call by at the weekend,” one joked. “Does it come with the neighbor who needs help with plumbing,” another said, referring to Mandy, who lives nearby in-game.

“After GTA VI, these houses will appreciate in value more than anywhere else in America,” another added.

There are plenty of real-life places that have inspired GTA 6 locations. It is a little freaky to be driving around Ocean Beach if you’ve actually been to Miami Beach.

The market is getting nervous with all the shenanigans involving Nvidia and Jensen Huang.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

S&P Global Ratings, one of the world’s largest rating agencies, announced that it’s putting Nvidia’s credit rating on watch as the company faces a possible downgrade. The agency says that the issues surrounding Jensen Huang regarding export control violations, as well as his resignation and subsequent reinstatement 11 days later, reveal the weakness of Nvidia’s management team and board oversight.

“We believe the long tenure of certain board members may have compromised their independence,” the rating agency said in its press release. It also added, “Issues related to Nvidia’s China exposure also raise questions regarding geopolitical risk management and internal controls. While Nvidia management has delivered significant returns for its stakeholders over decades, weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning have brought questions around the ‘key man’ risk to the forefront. The departure, then reinstatement of the CEO, does not sufficiently answer our questions regarding board oversight of executive code of conduct.”

Nvidia CEO and founder Jensen Huang resigned from the job on November 12, after multiple allegations of affairs, especially with Megaspeed CEO Alice Huang, who’s also accused of enabling the smuggling of $7.5 billion worth of AI GPUs into China. Even though Huang left the company he founded in disgrace, he was reinstated to the position by the Nvidia board on November 23. This was a major surprise, especially as it had previously refused to bring him back due to the ongoing national security investigation into Jensen.

However, it seems that Nvidia’s board of directors has folded to the pressure, especially after President Donald Trump himself said that he talked to them directly, with the board saying that it “received input from senior levels of the United States” that “Mr. Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.” Despite those assurances, the Nvidia chief still faces multiple investigations, including an internal inquiry at Nvidia, a private meeting with the House of Representatives’ Select Committee on China, and a public testimony in front of the Senate Banking Committee, among others.

Even though the AI chipmaker’s risk profile remains unchanged, S&P Global Ratings’ warning is a sign that the market is uneasy about the situation regarding stricter export controls and the allegations of chip diversion. The ratings agency said that the downgrade could happen in the next 90 days after it concludes its due diligence, although it expects any potential drop to “be limited to one notch, unless investigations or disclosures lead us to believe Nvidia’s reputation or future business prospects have been harmed.”

#10820. Quantum mechanics once baffled scientists. Now it's changing the world

For much of the early 20th century, quantum mechanics was one of the most puzzling ideas in science. The theory challenged conventional thinking and left even leading physicists struggling to make sense of its implications. A century later, it has become the foundation of technologies that influence daily life, including lasers, microchips, secure communications, and emerging quantum computers.

In a new perspective article published in Science, Dr. Marlan Scully of Texas A&M University reflects on the remarkable evolution of quantum mechanics, from an abstract theory about tiny particles to a powerful framework helping researchers tackle some of the most difficult questions in science.

"Quantum mechanics started as a way to explain the behavior of tiny particles," said Scully, who is also affiliated with Princeton University. "Now it's driving innovations that were unimaginable just a generation ago."

Scully has played a major role in advancing the field. He co-authored the influential textbook Quantum Optics, a resource that has educated generations of physicists. His research in coherent nanoscale laser spectroscopy helped make it possible to study molecules with atomic-scale precision. He has also developed groundbreaking concepts involving quantum heat engines, which challenge traditional assumptions about thermodynamic efficiency and may one day lead to new energy technologies.

From Schrödinger's Cat to Quantum Technology

One of the most famous illustrations of quantum mechanics came in 1935, when Erwin Schrödinger proposed his cat paradox. The thought experiment suggested that a cat could exist in a state that is both alive and dead until it is observed. Schrödinger introduced the idea to highlight how strange quantum theory appeared.

Today, those once-bizarre concepts have moved far beyond philosophical debates.

"That 'quantum weirdness' is no longer just a philosophical puzzle," said Scully. "It's the foundation of quantum computing, quantum cryptography and even gravitational wave detection."

The foundations of quantum mechanics were built by pioneers including Schrödinger and Werner Heisenberg. They developed two different mathematical approaches, wave mechanics and matrix mechanics, to describe quantum systems. Over time, these approaches were unified and contributed to the development of quantum field theory, which explains how particles interact through electromagnetic and nuclear forces.

Their work expanded on Niels Bohr's early atomic model, which pictured electrons orbiting the nucleus much like planets orbit the sun. While later discoveries refined that picture, Bohr's model helped pave the way for modern quantum theory.

Among the most important concepts in quantum mechanics is quantum coherence. This phenomenon allows particles such as atoms and photons to remain linked in a coordinated state, even across significant distances.

Quantum coherence led directly to the development of the laser, a technology that many once believed could never work. Today, lasers are used throughout modern society, from supermarket barcode scanners to vision correction procedures and advanced scientific instruments.

Coherence is also closely tied to quantum entanglement, the phenomenon that prompted Albert Einstein to describe it as "spooky action at a distance."

Entanglement enables particles to share information through uniquely quantum properties. These effects form the basis of quantum encryption systems and improve the sensitivity of sophisticated instruments such as the Laser Interferometer Gravitational-Wave Observatory (LIGO), which detects tiny ripples in spacetime.

Quantum Heat Engines Challenge Classical Limits

One of the more surprising applications of quantum physics involves quantum heat engines.

Traditional engines are constrained by the Carnot Limit, which defines the maximum efficiency allowed by classical thermodynamics. Researchers have found that by exploiting quantum coherence, it may be possible to create engines that exceed those classical limits.

"It's a striking example of how quantum principles can rewrite the rules of classical physics," Scully said.

The influence of quantum mechanics now extends far beyond physics.

In biology, techniques such as coherent Raman spectroscopy allow researchers to examine viruses and other structures at the nanoscale, providing valuable insights into the microscopic world.

Quantum ideas are also shaping efforts to understand the universe itself. Scientists working on concepts such as string theory and quantum gravity are attempting to reconcile quantum mechanics with Einstein's theory of relativity, one of the biggest unsolved problems in modern physics.

Researchers are even applying quantum concepts to the long-standing challenge of understanding turbulence. The chaotic motion of air and fluids affects weather patterns, climate systems, and aircraft performance. By studying superfluid helium, a substance that displays unusual quantum behavior, scientists are uncovering patterns that could improve climate modeling, storm prediction, and aviation safety.

Despite a century of success, quantum mechanics continues to raise profound questions.

Can gravity be quantized (i.e., does gravity behave like other forces at the quantum level)? Could quantum computers transform medicine and materials science? What new insights about the universe might emerge from future quantum technologies?

Scully believes the search for answers is only beginning.

"At the start of the 20th century, many thought physics was complete," he said. "Now, in the 21st century, we know the adventure is just beginning."

Five Ways Quantum Mechanics Affects Everyday Life

#10818. Scientists discovered the brain doesn't make decisions the way we thought

Scientists at the University of Illinois Urbana Champaign have uncovered evidence that could reshape how researchers think about both the brain and artificial intelligence. Their findings suggest that decision making begins much earlier in the brain than traditional theories propose, offering fresh ideas for designing future AI systems that are more capable and far more energy efficient.

Led by electrical and computer engineering professor Yurii Vlasov at The Grainger College of Engineering, the research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Science (PNAS). The study points to an unexpected role for early sensory brain regions in decision making, challenging the long accepted view that decisions emerge only after information moves through a strict hierarchy of brain regions.

The human brain is widely regarded as the most complex structure in the known universe. Scientists still do not fully understand how it works, which is why reverse engineering the brain was identified by the National Academy of Engineering in 2008 as one of the 14 grand challenges for engineering in the 21st century.

For decades, many artificial intelligence systems, including convolutional neural networks, have been inspired by the idea that the brain processes information in a one way sequence. According to this traditional model, sensory information travels upward through increasingly complex brain regions until it reaches the frontal cortex, where decisions are made.

Vlasov and other researchers have increasingly questioned whether that picture is complete.

Instead, they are exploring a model based on natural intelligence, which has been refined through evolution over hundreds of millions of years. In this framework, the brain does not rely only on a step by step flow of information. Decision making also depends on interconnected feedback loops that allow information to move in both directions between brain regions.

Because biological intelligence performs remarkably complex tasks while using far less energy than today's AI systems, understanding this architecture could help guide the development of future artificial intelligence.

"We want to learn from a billion years of evolution," Vlasov said. "How is that biological intelligence organized architecturally? Can we learn from the architectural side of the brain and emulate that to make AI more effective, less power hungry, and more intelligent than it currently is? In the level of decision-making, that's where current AI is lacking."

Early Brain Regions Show Decision Making Activity

To investigate how these processes work, the research team focused on the brain's earliest stages of sensing and perception.

The scientists recorded neural activity in mice as they navigated a virtual reality corridor and made perceptual decisions. They found evidence of decision related activity in the primary somatosensory cortex (S1), one of the brain's earliest sensory processing areas.

Rather than simply passing information forward, S1 appeared to be influenced by higher brain regions through feedback loops. This top down regulation suggests that decision making involves continuous communication across multiple brain areas instead of a simple one direction flow of information.

"The neural code of the brain is still mostly an unknown language," Vlasov said. "But this systems-level understanding can be viewed as a potential impact on how more efficient artificial neural networks can be built -- how the next generation of AI can be thought through. Maybe with these analogies that we learn from real brains, we can improve AI further."

What the Findings Could Mean for Future AI

The researchers emphasize that the study does not provide a blueprint for building better artificial intelligence. Instead, it offers new insights into how the brain organizes decision making that could eventually inspire future AI architectures.

Next, Vlasov and his team plan to investigate the timing of these brain signals in greater detail. They also intend to develop new technologies for measuring neural activity to better understand how feedback loops emerge and coordinate different levels of brain processing.

"By looking at the fast temporal dynamics of neural activity, maybe we can understand better how these feedback loops are engaged in making decisions," Vlasov said. "Maybe that's the approach that potentially uncovers these currently unknown mechanisms -- how these feedback loops are organized dynamically and how they form and shape different levels of processing. Maybe that can be implemented in new architectures for AI."

Materials provided by University of Illinois Grainger College of Engineering. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
